### Dr. Johann Salomo Semler’s
## Treatise
->on the free<-
# Investigation
### of the Canon;
<note>The "Canon" refers to the collection of books officially accepted as Holy Scripture.</note>
->together with an<-
## Answer
->to the<-
## Tübingen Defense
<note>This refers to a specific theological argument originating from the University of Tübingen, which defended the traditional view of the Bible against Semler's modern critiques.</note>
### of the Apocalypse.
<note>The "Apocalypse" refers to the Book of Revelation, the final book of the New Testament.</note>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page begins the formal dedicatory letter to Johann Friedrich Wilhelm Jerusalem, following the title-style dedication on the previous page. The author describes a transformative spiritual experience in Magdeburg involving prominent Enlightenment theologians.</meta>

# Most Reverend Lord Abbot,
## Highly Honored Patron!

If only I could successfully express something of the great and powerful feelings in this letter, which I address publicly to Your Reverence—feelings that I took with me from Magdeburg, when God granted me the great fortune to find new spiritual life <term>Spiritual life: In this context, "geistliches Leben" refers to a religious awakening or a deepening of faith, likely influenced by the German Enlightenment's focus on practical piety.</term> in the most select company of a Mr. Saks <note>Likely Friedrich Samuel Gottfried Sack (1738–1817), a prominent Reformed theologian and court preacher in Berlin.</note> and a Mr. Spalding <note>Johann Joachim Spalding (1714–1804), a leading figure of the "Neology" movement in German theology which sought to reconcile faith with reason.</note>, and now also, to be recommended and introduced to Your Reverence for the first time by these, shall I say, my fathers in Christ <note>original: "Väter in Christo"</note>, or to experience your own gracious condescension toward me directly! With what feeling, and hon— <note>The text breaks here; the German "ehr-" combined with the catchword "erbie-" suggests the word "ehrerbietigem" (honorable or respectful).</note>

<summary>In this dedicatory letter, the author expresses profound gratitude for his spiritual awakening in Magdeburg and his introduction to the Abbot through the influence of influential theologians Sack and Spalding.</summary>
<keywords>Magdeburg, Johann Joachim Spalding, Friedrich Samuel Gottfried Sack, spiritual life, patronage, Enlightenment theology, dedication</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedicatory letter to Abbot Jerusalem. It follows the author's description of his meeting with the theologians Sack and Spalding in Magdeburg, which he views as a pivotal spiritual encounter.</meta>

—orable silence, I was permeated there! But just as words almost completely failed me then, so it is for me now in this public letter; I must likewise count more upon your great, noble heart than upon any service I might expect from my own decorous skill. All Latin or scholarly art is, in any case, almost repugnant to me when I think of the <term>spiritual gift <note>original: χάρισma πνευματικόν (charisma pneumatikon). A New Testament term referring to a gift of grace or a spiritual endowment given by the Holy Spirit.</note></term> which I found in these estimable persons—and which, as I imagine, flowed quite easily from them down to me when they called me their friend! And truly, I think of it unceasingly, and for my great encouragement, I shall keep everything within a fine, good heart that I experienced there of divine power in life and conduct, and in worthy, great judgments. Already I have praised this far and wide; not because you three <note>Refers to the recipient, Abbot Jerusalem, and the previously mentioned Sack and Spalding.</note> seemed to attach any value to me—even if I, as such a modest beginner in this noble <term>school of the spirit <note>This phrase suggests the 18th-century "theology of experience," where faith was viewed not as a set of dogmas to be memorized, but as an internal education or development of the soul.</note></term>, could have tolerated it—but because truly God was among us, and that we [honored/glorified] His great name from the heart... <note>The text cuts off here at the syllable "ver-"; the catchword "herr-" on the original indicates the word "verherrlichten," meaning glorified or praised.</note>

<summary>The author describes the profound emotional and spiritual impact of his meeting with leading theologians, choosing to write from the heart rather than using academic artifice. He interprets their friendship as a "spiritual gift" and a sign of God's presence among them.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual gift, charisma pneumatikon, school of the spirit, Christian fellowship, German Enlightenment, piety, religious experience</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>The author continues his introductory remarks, reflecting on his personal spiritual renewal and his reception among esteemed Christian circles despite his controversial reputation.</meta>

...glorified; often in a considerable number of noble Christians, including many <term>chosen ladies<note>original Greek: "kyriōn eklektōn." A reference to 2 John 1:1, implying women of high social and spiritual standing.</note></term>. I cannot do otherwise; I must declare it to them: I have begun my <term>professional work<note>Berufsarbeit: referring here to his clerical or academic duties as a divine calling.</note></term> again with new strength. I believe that my listeners have likely even heard from me what sort of occasion it was that so powerfully and piercingly fired me up anew toward the greatest possible faithfulness and all seriousness in my calling. For no other way remains for me to worthily thank God for this, and to rightly apply His great benefits, than that I myself seek to walk more and more in the true power of the Spirit of Christ—which such great men, as an example to me and others, allowed to be perceived so greatly and brilliantly in themselves! 

Indeed, I truly wondered with joy that I—whom others, <term>those who seem to be pillars<note>original Greek: "hoi dokountes styloi einai." A citation of Galatians 2:9, referring to established church leaders.</note></term>, so easily call a deliberate heretic and almost a daring Saul—was received so lovely in this select company; unless, in that constant stirring of my heart, I allowed many a wise rebuke to pass by entirely unnoticed...

<summary>The author describes a spiritual reawakening in his professional calling inspired by a group of elite Christians, noting the irony of being welcomed by them while established church leaders label him a heretic.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual renewal, vocation, heresy, ecclesiastical authority, chosen ladies, pillars of the church, Saul of Tarsus</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the author's introductory address, following the catchword "dre" (likely from "drehen" or "drein") from the previous page.</meta>

...to be turned. However, I must not have appeared so evil and malicious to your eyes at that time, if you still wished to improve me so gently and so kindly.

But it must now seem all the more strange that I embolden myself to publicly dedicate this small treatise <note>German: "Abhandlung"</note> to you, my heart's quite invaluable patron <note>German: "Gönner"</note>: a work in which I present and partially resolve, with great freedom, several theological problems <note>German: "theologische Aufgaben"</note> which certainly have not been common among academic theologians <note>German: "academischen Theologis"</note> until now. I have no excuse other than that which may be grounded in my good conscience, my honest—even if perhaps incorrect—understanding <note>German: "Erkenntnis"</note>, and my integrity. Accordingly, I would very gladly allow myself to be taught otherwise by such great men as those I have just named, among whom the worthy and incomparable **Ernesti** <note>Johann August Ernesti (1707–1781), a leading German rationalist theologian and philologist</note> certainly still belonged. With such a work, I can surely seek nothing other than the true and consequently easier recommendation of the Christian doctrine <note>German: "christliche Lehre"</note> and our own religion among our contemporaries; everything else, truly everything else...

<summary>The author dedicates his theological treatise to a patron, defending his unconventional approach to religious problems as an honest attempt to make Christian doctrine more accessible to his contemporaries.</summary>
<keywords>theology, dedication, Johann August Ernesti, Christian doctrine, academic theologians, enlightenment, religious reform</keywords>

Since Your Excellency has already bestowed upon us so many public benefits through your valuable writings—which have as their goal this same easier and certain recommendation of our so powerful and noble religion—I may perhaps, through this small essay, prompt all the more thorough decisions on many of the tasks I have presented; decisions which the spirit of a <note>The author is likely referring to Johann Friedrich Wilhelm Jerusalem (1709–1789), a key figure of the German Enlightenment and court preacher.</note> Jerusalem can grant regarding many difficulties, far more engagingly and validly than several other writers combined, who have long been maintaining a constant clamor against me.

nyfius, which has found much applause until now. I also have the heart to prophesy that it will no longer maintain the same prestige that it sought to give itself through exceptional arrogance. You likely do not know, most worthy patron, that I have already been struck more than once with the orthodox ban and anathema <note>original: "Anathema"; a formal curse or excommunication by a church authority</note>; that they even tell students <note>original: "Studiofis"; likely a transcription of "Studiosis," meaning university students</note> quite well-meaningly that I will come to a terrifying end—I know not under which trumpet blast. I do not fear such old prophetic formulas at all, even if they are regarded by the simple-minded almost as miraculous gifts <note>original: "Wundergaben"</note>, concerning which I do not hold the old church opinion <note>original: "Kirchenmeinung"</note> either. However, frivolous people, who lightheadedly fit themselves into such affected prophetic garb and sheep's clothing <note>original: "Schaafspelze"</note>, make it their business through their incessant mockery not only to despise that presumed infallibility, but also—in a very natural connection—to accidentally cause much harm to the certain and serious doctrine of religion <note>original: "Religion"</note> among others to whom they recount such interviews.

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>The previous page ended with the catchword "übri-" (remaining/rest), suggesting a transition from a discussion of remaining theological points to the author's core intentions.</meta>

My intention is, as I have said, entirely honest and sincere; those things which are not Christian dogmas in themselves—according to their content and their true, authentic basis of knowledge—I cannot regard as such. This is how I judge the current theory of the <term>Canon<note>The official list of books accepted as Holy Scripture.</note></term>, because it allows for no real utility; and people have confused the *canon of churchmen* <note>original: "canonem ecclesiasticorum hominum"</note> or of former church servants with the *canon of Christians* <note>original: "canone christianorum"</note>. Share with me, most worthy Lord Abbot, according to your upright integrity, your own insights; I am happy to be refuted, but I will not allow myself to be shouted down, branded a heretic, or cursed <note>original: "anathematisiren"</note>; no more than Luther allowed himself to be by the *orthodox doctors of Louvain* <note>original: "louanienses orthodoxos doctores"</note>. This is so that the old, base theological servitude and *servitude to men* <note>original: "ανθρωποδελεια" (anthropodouleia)</note> does not further damage the power and worthy content of the Christian, blameless, and inoffensive doctrine and its easy application. You are not among such merciless *inquisitors of heretical depravity* <note>original: "inquisitoribus haereticae prauitatis"</note>; you can no more be so than other great men who do not make a <term>Compendium<note>A concise summary or textbook of theology, often used rigidly in the 18th century to enforce orthodoxy.</note></term> into their sidearm. I believe

<summary>The author appeals to a high-ranking Abbot for an honest intellectual exchange, arguing that the traditional biblical canon has been conflated with mere ecclesiastical tradition. <keywords>Biblical Canon, Church Authority, Martin Luther, Louvain theologians, Theological Freedom, Inquisitors, Dogma, 18th-century theology</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>The catchword "lehr-" suggests the text continues onto the next page with a word like "lehrreiche" (instructive) or "Lehrart" (method of teaching).</meta>

Moreover, I least of all claim that I hit the mark so easily in everything; and what man is ever judged so strictly? I have indeed often failed, more often than has perhaps been pointed out to me; and I may fail further. That which I have not hit upon, sensible readers or scholars <term>Gelehrte: the educated elite of the 18th century</term> should and certainly will avoid, and they will surely not credit it to me as a merit <note>original: "Merite"</note>; but I am not thereby a blasphemer <note>original: "Gotteslästerer"</note>; I am a fellow-worker, whom some surpass in skill, many in imagination, but very few in diligence and honesty. 

But where is the theological prudence <note>original: "theologische Klugheit"; refers to the cautious, diplomatic way of handling sensitive religious topics</note> in such freedom of writing? Here I am again a heretic <note>original: "Ketzer"</note> along with Luther; as a teacher at academies <note>universities</note>, I cannot count silence as prudence, but rather as convenience and the fear of man <note>original: "Menschenfurcht"; a biblical concept of prioritizing human approval over divine truth</note>; however, I also wish to learn true wisdom more and more. Although, since almost all theologians <note>original: "Theologi"</note> are so very cautious: I should not think that through the haste or boldness of a single author (whom many, in any case, diligently judge with great art, asserting he is not without error) and through my scholarly—

Everyone helps to defend and offer resistance; what then will one man alone accomplish against it? Especially if he does not have the truth on his side at all?

I have long confessed that the <term>compendia</term><note>Handbooks or summaries of Christian doctrine.</note> and <term>systems</term><note>Comprehensive theological frameworks used for teaching.</note> no longer seem good or sufficient to me for the current purpose; the doctrine of <term>Holy Scripture</term><note>original: "de scriptura sacra"</note> must primarily be reworked much better and more correctly; of course, people will immediately make a great assault upon me with the old complaints about "evil reason." But these old Latin confusions of <term>Augustine</term><note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), whose views on original sin and grace dominated Western theology.</note> and many others, who likely meant better than he did, will also gradually be unraveled. Certainly, man's understanding, reason, will, and the use of all his human faculties are brought into a new order and skill through the <term>Word of God</term>; this is also necessary for all people; but <term>Holy Scripture</term>, taken as a single volume, is not directly this powerful Word of God that improves mankind. --- Page 18 ---
<meta>The catchword "gewis" (certainly) from the previous page likely concludes a sentence regarding the certainty of faith or scripture, leading into the discussion of those who lack its power.</meta>

there is no shortage of Jews and even of Christians who experience so little of the power of the Word of God in their understanding and will toward their own improvement, even though many can recite the <term>Holy Scripture<note>original: "scripturam sacram"</note></term> almost by heart. In short, I submit myself to the examination and instruction of all reasonable and pious theologians, as well as all thinking and searching Christians; and I am quite willing to learn more.

Just as I was nearly finished with the following treatise, the worthy Dr. Töllner’s <note>Johann Gottlieb Töllner (1724–1774), a German theologian known for his moderate Enlightenment views on biblical inspiration.</note> new and detailed writing on **Inspiration** came into my hands. This learned man has certainly expressed and investigated many things better, and he also does not yet seem to go as far as I do; I will therefore also instruct myself with much profit from this writing. I will likely not lack for many other actual opponents anyway; perhaps I shall also learn something good from them, whether it be said to me rudely and maliciously or in a friendly manner.

People will be startled first and most of all as to whether I do not, in this manner, weaken or even subvert the so-called <term>principle of knowledge<note>original: "principium cognoscendi." In theology, this refers to the source or foundation—usually Scripture—from which religious truths are derived.</note></term> and...

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>The catchword "sie" (they) from the previous page connects to the opening sentence regarding the confirmation or abolition of certain principles.</meta>

...certainly establishes or abolishes. However, it is nothing less than that; one must simply not take offense that I claim regarding several books that many readers—perhaps more capable or more practiced ones—might not consider them at all as sources from which they should take further moral instruction for their improvement. On the other hand, such books can still be kept and utilized by other readers, or by other people who possess a lesser degree of knowledge. Some moral ideas and truths thus become known to some readers for the first time through these works; whereas, for other people, these truths are actually already better known and are, or were, more present in their daily practice than they ever appear in such books. In this case—if, for example, a pious Jew on his travels shared his knowledge with many a crude pagan—the pagan accepted them without asking further for a <term>written principle of knowing<note>original: "principio scripto sciendi"; refers to a formal, documented source of authority or a philosophical foundation for knowledge.</note></term>. In the same way, when Paul preached, he made truths known that are called partly historical and partly moral; these latter truths carry an immediate effect for the listener within themselves, while the historical ones have a mediated effect when they...

<summary>The author discusses how the moral value of a book depends on the reader's existing knowledge and argues that moral truths can be intuitively accepted without formal written proof, citing the examples of Jewish travelers and the Apostle Paul.</summary>

Now, all the books that possess such instructional content, either in their entirety or in their individual parts, truly remain a <term>principle of knowledge <note>original: "principium cognoscendi"</note></term>, both for the teacher and for every listener who thinks along with them. --- Page 21 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the limitations of interpreting scripture; the catchword "nie" (never) suggests a continuation of a negative or restrictive statement regarding how the text should be approached.</meta>

one should not understand it merely as a historical source (from which, certainly, the history of the Jews is learned); even less as a guide for action or practice <note>original: "agendi oder praxeos"</note>, even if a so-called **Moralization** <note>The practice of interpreting stories as allegories for moral behavior.</note> is brought in to help. For the latter merely inserts those "better truths" and thus assembles a different content and understanding of the book. Our entire instruction regarding spiritual truths, the nature of sinful or incorrect actions, the abolition of this poor state, and the new inner skill to apply this knowledge ever better: all of this is taken solely from this principle of knowledge <note>original: "principio cognoscendi"</note> which was previously made known among Jews or Christians, and it retains this source. The specific ideas of GOD the Father, of the Son of GOD, and of the Holy Spirit—through which authors the existing knowledge and effects have been brought about—always distinguish the Christian religion from all others; just as the Christian parties <note>Referring to different denominations or sects.</note> always distinguish themselves from one another in the very specific details of these concepts regarding these Christian subjects, so that

they are connected with moral concepts regarding the purpose of these past events. Now, all books that possess such instructional content, whether in their entirety or in individual parts, truly remain a <term>principle of knowledge <note>original: "principium cognoscendi"; the source or basis by which we come to know a truth</note></term>, both for the teacher and for every listener who thinks along with them. And these very truths are and remain, then, also directly the divine <term>principle of practice <note>original: "principium praxeos"; the basis for moral action or religious conduct</note></term>, just as they carried divine effects with them at that time through the author of the speech or action. However, if in books or their parts no such teachings and instructions for inner spiritual improvement appear, but merely human actions and histories: then they are indeed included under the name <term>Sacred Scripture <note>original: "scriptura sacra"</note></term>; but they do not, for that reason, belong to the <term>principle of knowledge</term> (if

Since I require these truths for my own spiritual restoration, and have experienced their divine power to my great advantage and inner peace, I am all the more certain that I have not written here out of levity, or even out of curiosity and a craving for novelty <note>original: "Fürwitz und Kizel"; refers to an intellectual restlessness or "itching" for new ideas</note>. I seek to make that which is so useful and certain to me easier for others to use for their own happiness; least of all do I intend to cause any harm.

I publicly ask for the kind continuation of the goodwill and love once granted to me; I would express myself more strongly if I did not know that my particular inner feelings cannot be so easily described when I cannot call upon the additional assistance of your presence. What else does a truth-loving, rational person seek even now, according to his somewhat brighter and larger horizon of vision, than precisely this inner peace and gentle conviction? He seeks to find that those universal, infallible truths and concepts of God—of His real, great perfections and intentions for humanity—can truly be discovered in an undeniable and easy connection with the specific content of a book claimed to be of divine origin. We are speaking here of those contemporaries who are not, and do not remain, mere beginners in Christianity; we speak of the special right of such Christians who truly wish to use their faculties, and who thereby—to be sure—can make very different observations and perceptions regarding one and the same subject than most other so-called Christians are capable of. To approve principles for one's own **moral** state as a whole, which could bring with it the greatest perfection of the powers of the soul: one can still do this even now; someone can do it with the very same good conscience; it can truly help toward that same great and good end, so that such a selective reader indeed becomes more adept and capable of all good works, all virtues, and excellences; which is the ultimate goal and consequence of all properly grounded and rational religions, and even more so of the Christian one. # Of the Canon.

The term was used for instruction. The meaning of **canonical books** has likewise been changed gradually over time. In the older eras, it is quite undeniable that "Canon" refers to the list of books which were publicly read aloud in the gatherings of Christians; canonical writings, or books, are precisely those which are intended for public reading. Therefore, even the letters or essays of living bishops could be parts of this <term>canonical reading<note>original: "canonica lectione"</note></term>. Thus, those very writings were included among them which were later distinguished by the name **apocrypha** from those which were called divine or inspired by God. For example, the <term>Catechumens<note>Candidates for baptism receiving basic instruction in the Christian faith.</note></term> were read the moral books—Sirach, Wisdom, Tobit—as well as the letters of Clement, the writings of Ephrem, and the stories of saints or martyrs, etc. Such moral writings as those of Ephrem, and the stories of martyrs and saints, were read to the rest of the Christians just as much as the <term>circular letters<note>original: "Circularschreiben"</note></term> of many bishops; just as Augustine even introduced the reading of the <term>Acts of the Conference of Carthage<note>original: "gesta collationis carthaginiensis"; a record of the 411 AD meeting between Catholic and Donatist bishops.</note></term> in the African churches during Lent; and the accounts of the discovered relics of Saint Stephen, and of the conversion of the Jews in Minorca, etc., were read in the churches. Baronius relates the matter clearly <term>at the year 418, number 43<note>Refers to the "Annales Ecclesiastici" by Caesar Baronius, a massive history of the church.</note></term>; regarding the letters

<summary>This page explains that the term "canonical" originally referred to any text authorized for public reading in church, including non-biblical works like martyr stories and episcopal letters, before the term was narrowed to mean divinely inspired scripture.</summary>

->From the circular letters of Bishop Severus in Minorca, and from Blessed Evodius’s book *On the Miracles of Saint Stephen*:<-

"On the same day that the relics of Blessed Stephen entered the church, at the very beginning of the canonical readings, a letter from Bishop Severus was brought to us; it was read aloud from the pulpit into the ears of the church with great favor—whereupon, through this reading of his deeds, as if he were extending his own hands to the cheering and exulting faithful, he himself seemed to say: 'Behold, you have the martyr,' etc." <note>original: "eodem die, quo ingressae sunt ecclesiam b. Stephani reliquiae... ecce habetis martyrem etc." This passage describes the liturgical practice of reading accounts of martyrs alongside scripture.</note>

In order to maintain uniformity and agreement regarding these books—which were used in the churches for reading and preaching—and to distinguish oneself clearly enough from the so-called heretical parties in reading, just as in singing and the liturgy: the bishops of several churches and provinces finally united with one another, partly orally and partly in writing, to determine which books from the so-called Old and New Testaments should constitute the <term>canonical reading</term> <note>Latin: *canonicam lectionem*; refers to the officially sanctioned list of books allowed to be read during public worship</note>. One need only keep in mind certain passages such as those appearing in the African Councils of the 4th century; for example, in the year 397, at the Third Council of Carthage, Canon 47: "It was decided that besides the canonical scriptures, nothing shall be read in the church (the emphasis is on the public reading in the church) under the name of divine scriptures; the canonical scriptures, however, are: Genesis, Exodus..." <note>original: "placuit, vt praeter scripturas canonicas, nihil in ecclesia legatur... sunt autem canonicae scripturae: Genesis, Exodus"</note>

# Of the Canon.

Leviticus, Numbers, Deuteronomy, Joshua <note>original: "Iesus Naue"</note>, Judges, Ruth, 4 books of Kings <note>In older systems, 1 & 2 Samuel and 1 & 2 Kings were grouped as the four books of Kings</note>, 2 books of Chronicles <note>original: "paralipomenon"</note>, Job, one book of Psalms, five books of Solomon <note>original: "Salomonis libri quinque"</note>, <insert>with us later only 3.</insert> the twelve books of the minor prophets; likewise Isaiah, Jeremiah, Ezekiel, Daniel, Tobit, Judith, 2 books of Ezra, 2 books of Maccabees. Of the New Testament, however: 4 books of the Gospels, one book of the Acts of the Apostles, 13 epistles of Paul the Apostle, one of the same to the Hebrews; two of Peter the Apostle; three of John the Apostle; one of Jude the Apostle; and one of James. One book of the Revelation <note>original: "Apocalypsis"</note> of John. Now follows further: <note>The following is a Latin decree from the Council of Carthage, 397 AD</note> "Let this also be made known to our brother and fellow-priest Boniface (in Rome), or to other bishops of those parts, for the purpose of confirming the canon: because we have received from the fathers that these are the books to be read in the church. It is also permitted that the passions of the Martyrs be read when their anniversary days are celebrated." Just so writes Innocent I <note>Bishop of Rome from 401–417 AD</note> to Bishop Exsuperius of Toulouse, who had inquired about various matters. The final answer is: "Which books are truly received (namely in Rome at that time) into the canon of the Holy Scriptures: the attached brief <note>original: "Breuis"; this means an enclosed notice or summary</note> shows. These are, therefore, the things which you wished to be advised of by voice (or in turn?): 5 books of Moses, that is, Genesis etc., as well as Joshua and Judges, and 4 books of Kings; together with Ruth. 16 books of the Prophets."

->§. 4.<-

According to these very clear circumstances, it is certain that the common notion of the constant uniformity and equality of the Canon—or the list of public holy scriptures of the Christians—is without foundation or historical accuracy, if it is <gloss>under</gloss>stood <note>original: "perstanden," a variation of "verstanden"</note> as anything other than an agreement for public society and the public practice of religion, by which individual reflective Christians did not always consider themselves bound. The ecclesiastical societies either had a different custom in this regard, according to which they actually differed from other churches in the use of such writings for public reading and for religious establishment; or they had gradually agreed and united with many or few others for that reason. One must look at both aspects here: the public reading, and the diversity of the books used for it, until gradually a common agreement was reached.

Canonicity <note>The status of a book being accepted as official scripture</note> was therefore established in regard to the clergy <note>original: "Clericorum"</note>, meaning that they were permitted to use no other books for public reading or for binding instruction than those which were reckoned as canonical books <note>original: "canonicis libris"</note> within that specific church. The distinction between the older parties among the Christians always relates to their differing canonical books; just as later, once the ecclesiastical canon <note>original: "canon ecclesiasticus"</note> was established, the distinction was based on the interpretation and application of the content of these now-accepted books. Therefore **Tertullian** <note>A prolific early Christian author from Carthage, c. 155–220 AD</note> admits, for example: that Gnostic parties did not accept the four **Gospels** <note>original: "Evangelia"</note> accepted by other Christians; that **Marcion** <note>An early Christian theologian whose teachings led to a major alternative church</note> used a historical treatise on the life and teachings of Christ which was very different from our **Gospels**, though it agreed most closely with the treatise of **Luke**, yet with the omission of many reports concerning the birth of **Jesus** and other circumstances that were primarily related to Jewish readers and listeners. The **Gnostics** <note>A diverse group of early religious thinkers who emphasized secret knowledge (gnosis) for salvation</note> also accepted neither our Acts of the Apostles nor this Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation</note>, nor all the letters of Paul, and even these they did not have with completely identical content. Another party, called the Jewish-thinking Christians <note>Likely referring to groups such as the Ebionites</note>, were enemies of all the writings of Paul; and they therefore had books containing narratives of the teachings and travels of Peter, intended for his particular praise

As briefly as I touch upon this here, it is demonstrably true when history is consulted. This diversity appeared very early on, alongside the emergence and spread of specific sects. Therefore, when one speaks of the agreement of Christians regarding the books they accepted as the foundation of their system of doctrine and the religion derived from it, one must properly understand: (1) the public system of doctrine, and (2) the larger portion of Christians, who are therefore called <term>katholikoi</term> <note>original Greek: καθολικοι; meaning "universal" or "general"</note>. It was this "catholic" or larger party that was unanimous in having such books read aloud publicly. Regarding other books, however, even within this party, no agreement existed in the older times; not until a more general connection of the churches across several provinces was gradually established. This greater unification regarding the public, external foundation of doctrine only took place from the 4th century onwards, when the practice of the Christian religion—owing to the superior number of members of this religious society—attained public legal status and equal freedom of public practice within the Roman state. From this point forward, the uniformity and unchangeability of the <term>canon</term> <note>the official list of inspired books</note> undeniably rests upon <term>public ecclesiastical law</term> <note>original Latin: iure ecclesiastico publico</note>; for this reason, more learned teachers

Teachers now distinguish their particular insights from the established common <term>system of doctrine<note>original: "Lehrordnung" — the standardized set of theological principles used for instruction</note></term>; and for the sake of this order, they write <term>commentaries<note>original: "Commentaria"</note></term> and deliver sermons on most of these books, the use of which had already been introduced for public reading; although many writings were not used at home by more practiced Christians, but rather others were used instead.

It is therefore correct and historically true: since the 4th and 5th centuries, the Canon—or the list of public writings of the Christians—has not been subject to further dispute or doubt among the <term>Catholics<note>In this 18th-century context, "catholicis" refers to the universal, mainstream church body before the later denominational splits.</note></term>; however, the immediate reason for this is the collective agreement of the bishops. This freedom could not be abolished by this Canon introduced for public use; for it is a very different objective that was intended and could be achieved through the ecclesiastical decree concerning the number of books belonging to public use and public religious practice. # Of the Canon.

A diversity presents itself among the readers who find themselves engaging with these texts. Just as it is clear from the accounts of the Evangelists <note>The authors of the four Gospels: Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John.</note> that JESUS adjusted his discourse and the measure of his instruction according to the very different capacities of those who listened to him at any given time: so it is quite certain that a writer also primarily sought to be useful to his readers according to their circumstances in the arrangement of his narrative or his instruction. Consequently, he has, as it were, not written many things with regard to others who already surpass his current readers in capacity and practice. Even more so, these readers retain the right not to exchange their good insights and principles—which are already truly useful to themselves and others—for those that offer them no greater advantage or public benefit.

If it were primarily a matter of personal acquaintance with the Apostles, then every party used to name certain individuals from whom their reports were supposed to borrow just as much authority of credibility as was claimed by the *Catholici* <note>original: "Catholicis"; referring here to the universal or mainstream early church rather than the modern Roman Catholic Church</note>. Let us assume a curious traveler asks with the most sincere intentions among the majority of Jews in Palestine, in Jerusalem: they all tell him of the 24 Hebrew books; if he goes among the Samaritans, they point him only to the 5 Books of Moses <note>The Pentateuch or Torah</note>, and are very dissatisfied with those other Jews; if he goes to Alexandria, he hears of even more holy books than those two parties have. Here it is certain that it really could not be decided through mere historical report and the indication that "these are divine books," and that "no more and no less must be accepted by upright lovers of the truth, or of the best religion." All testimony of the so-called Jewish and Christian Church is therefore insufficient for a seeker of truth to reach his own conviction regarding such books that are supposed to come from God; he hears a triple or quadruple account of one and the same matter,

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 28 regarding the nature of historical evidence and the formation of the biblical canon. The catchword "schie" from the previous page completes the word "Kirche" (Church) at the start of this page.</meta>

# Of the Canon.

church. He learns what these parties themselves say and believe; but through this, he has not become certain of the matter itself—of the actual divinity <note>The author distinguishes between historical facts and the spiritual "divinity" of a text.</note>. Just as those three, four, or five parties are quite convinced that each of them, to the exclusion of the others, possesses the most correct and complete report of it: so the particular conviction of this impartial researcher cannot be called directly subject to a foreign conviction or supposed certainty, no matter how great it may be. The testimony of the so-called Church can therefore, like any historical testimony, only affirm or deny something regarding an external event. However, the question of whether one or more books have a divine origin, or were inspired <term>inspired: the theological concept of "divine breath" or guidance in writing</term> to an author, is not an external event; therefore, there is no external or historical testimony for it. It can always be made historically true that the Samaritans accepted only the five books of Moses; that Jews in Palestine or Assyria accepted only twenty-four books, but Greek Jews accepted even more such books for their religious establishment, and are accustomed to accepting them: but that these books had a divine origin is not an object of history, which rests on the testimonies of other people in the same way as the destruction of Jerusalem, or the celebration of the Passover festival and the like. <meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the historical development of the Biblical canon and the interpretive methods used by Jewish and early Christian scholars.</meta>

# Of the Canon.

When nations adopt the **cultivation of the intellect** <note>German: "Cultur des Verstandes"; referring to the Enlightenment process of applying reason to traditional beliefs</note> and honestly declare what they now think, they abandon those principles regarding the established divinity and excellence of every individual part of their ancient holy books. Instead, they seek a true character of divinity within an instructional framework. They embed general moral concepts beneath **allegories** in those ancient narratives, even in the first book of Moses <note>The Book of Genesis</note>; as can be seen in the many writings that bear the name of **Philo**; not to mention many other names now, such as **Numenius** and **Aristobulus**, who sought similar ideas within those histories after they had exercised the capacity of their intellect through Pythagorean, Platonic, and other learning. Even the **Talmudists** and **Rabbis** would have had nothing to teach if they had not helped themselves through allegories.

The taste of the Greek Jews for allegories was subsequently most widely spread in **Alexandria**, and it contributed in no small measure to the fact that the Christians of the Catholic party <note>Referring here to the "universal" or mainstream early church</note> held all books and all parts of the so-called Old Testament in equal value and sought to connect them with their New Testament. Some other Jews, from the native Pharisaic party, have—under the name <term>akrobasis</term> <note>original Greek: ἀκρόβασις; literally "a stepping up," here referring to a deeper or mystical interpretation of the text</note>—sought general truths of **physics**, **cosmology**, **pneumatology** <note>The study of spiritual beings or the soul</note>, etc., beneath those narratives

<summary>This page discusses how Jewish and early Christian scholars used allegorical interpretation to reconcile ancient scriptures with Greek philosophy and rational thought. It highlights the influence of Alexandrian scholars and the Pharisaic tradition in finding deeper scientific and moral meanings within the biblical text.</summary>

<keywords>allegory, Philo of Alexandria, Hellenistic Judaism, Biblical Canon, Pharisaic tradition, Hermeneutics, Greek philosophy, Old Testament history</keywords>

# On Free Inquiry

...accustomed themselves to thinking; while only the common masses retained everything without distinction. Nevertheless, the majority of Christians gradually adopted all these books of the Jews as well—once people no longer looked toward the noble advancement of the **moral well-being** <term>moralisches Beste: the ethical improvement and spiritual health of the individual</term> of Christians, who were not meant to be mere **spiritual slaves**. These books were accepted as universal components of a complete divine instruction, which supposedly must always be used and accepted for all people of all times, regardless of their own existing better preparations for noble insights and greater moral precepts. Thus, people have asserted with more than Jewish zeal: that all these books, all their parts and pieces of content, are the written Word of God—the immediate revelation upon which the present and future spiritual perfection for each and every human being truly and constantly depends.

One could quite rightly retain and cherish as divine truths the undeniable principles of true spiritual perfection and the improvement of mankind, which are not uncommon in these books—especially in the **Psalms** and in other instructional books, such as parts of **Proverbs**, **Ecclesiastes**, **Job**, and the **Prophets**. These were certainly from God! But it did not follow that all twenty-four books <note>The twenty-four books of the Hebrew Bible (Tanakh)</note> were therefore, in all their content, nothing but such divine instruction; nor was it for all...

# On Free Inquiry

Meanwhile, despite all the spiritual blindness and quite coarse ignorance that one must attribute to the learned Jews when viewed as a whole, they possessed enough insight that they made a distinction regarding the old, customary idioms found in many narratives of those events. When these phrases could be considered offensive or scandalous in later times, they used expressions in their place during public reading that possessed more decency for that era. Furthermore, they actually did not read many histories and passages publicly at all, skipping over them entirely to avoid the offense that might arise even among their very unskilled listeners. For readers who wish to know this more precisely, I will transcribe this passage here from **Johann Morinus's** *Biblical Exercises*, Book 2, latter part, Exercise 22, Chapter 6 <note>Jean Morin (1591–1659) was a French biblical scholar; his *Exercitationes Biblicae* (1633) argued that the Hebrew text had been corrupted and praised the Samaritan Pentateuch.</note>, which, as far as I can judge, pertains primarily to my purpose. Thus **Morinus** writes on page 589: "I wish to advise you here of the public custom of the Jews." <note>original: "Consuetudinis Iudaeorum publicae hic te monitum volo."</note>

Thus they decree *concerning the scriptures which were customarily read in the synagogue*: the Rabbis taught that there are some scriptural texts which are read and explained (this is one class); some are read but not explained (this is the second class); some are neither read nor explained, etc. (this is the third class). # Of the Canon.

It is a concern to let people, especially our children, read all such narratives without any distinction. It is true that one can create some benefit for them through certain explanations and new instruction; however, this benefit does not arise at all from those stories of the lives of the Patriarchs <note>The biblical ancestors, such as Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob.</note>, or of Samson, etc., but rather from the instruction itself. One can, however, create an opportunity for instruction in a much more skillful manner. It is assumed that all these books, and all their parts, have a divine origin and exist as a revelation of God, consequently possessing a universal purpose. This I consider to be simply unprovable. The Old <insert>Covenant</insert> is passed away; everything was meant to become new and much more perfect than is possible if one is to recommend all these books as homogeneous parts of a whole—as equally good means to one and the same great end goal—to each and every beginner and practitioner alike, or to impress upon them an equal obligation toward them! Instruction in the most excellent Christianity can be arranged much more easily and directly, and much more usefully, than through such a burdensome retinue of ideas, which indeed always provide the foundation for Judaism, with all its inherent defects and obstacles, but not the foundation of Christianity.

I do not consider the instructional evidence for all twenty-four books of the Jews to be convincing or easily established; for the supporting arguments regarding many of these books are not arranged in a particularly orderly fashion. ->§. 16.<-

When one examines their content more closely, the so-called **Prophets**—a name under which the Jews also include the larger historical books following the Books of Moses—are likewise primarily intended for the Jews. This applies partly to all of them before the division of the kingdom took place, and partly specifically for the subjects of the Kingdom of Judah. # On Free Inquiry

Otherwise, people could not acquire such insights at all, or not as well, and could not utilize them so easily, if the content of these very books were not made known to all people. I believe, rather, that much more fruitful and useful histories and examples of actions—not merely of so-called rulers of a people, but primarily also of common members and citizens of a state—could be collected today for healthy instruction; wherein the **individual details**, which fit or belong only to this land of Palestine, would not so generally prevail, and would not bring with them so many particularly burdensome explanations and clarifications. 

The authors of such essays are called among the Jews **Nebiim** <note>original: "Nebiim"; the plural form of Prophet in Hebrew</note>; a **Nabi** <note>original: "Nabi"; a Prophet</note>; and the **Nebuah** <note>original: "Nebuah"; Prophecy or the prophetic gift</note> signifies a gift or skill in such pronouncements, **decisions**, announcements, and judgments that relate to the political and moral condition of the commonwealth, in order to improve and preserve it, and to communicate the necessary motives for rulers and subjects through hope and confidence, or through fear and threats. The content of the pronouncements is therefore primarily directed toward this civil **society** <note>original: "Societät"</note>, which should take reasons for lawful or advantageous behavior from the publicized pronouncements, whether for the present or the future. Therefore, the objects and parts of the description or the content are quite undeniably peculiar to this state,

Many of these <term>Nebiim</term> <note>Hebrew for "Prophets"</note> were simultaneously historians or authors of the annals, as well as of the reigns of individual kings; for this reason, Richard Simon <note>A 17th-century French priest regarded as a father of modern biblical criticism</note> has already judged that they represented, as it were, the position of secretaries of state. They transform all their ancient history, as it were, into nothing but miracles and the preoccupations of God with their people; because God, in their opinion, has nothing to do with the <term>Goim</term> <note>Hebrew for "Gentiles" or non-Jewish nations</note>, or other peoples, but has rather subjected them to other angels and rulers. Many of these <term>Nebiim</term>, however, honestly opposed the common superstition and the fanatical imagination of their people, and described and defended universal moral truths in their divine scope, in their sublime purpose, and in their healing power in such a way: that they taught publicly from time to time that all peoples on the whole earth should receive a share in this

Indeed, it is utterly vexing even to recount such follies. Nevertheless, even today among us—namely among Christians—people still search for such European, or Northern, or German calendars in these prophets or extraordinary teachers of the **Jews**! Regarding that divinity or inspiration which the Jews, according to the differences of their various parties <note>The author refers to the Pharisees, Sadducees, and other sects who held differing views on which books were authoritative.</note>, were accustomed to acknowledge, there is no doubt that Jesus and the Apostles actually presupposed this, and thus disputed with the Jews *ex concessis* <note>Latin: "from what has been conceded." A rhetorical strategy where one argues based on premises already accepted by the opponent.</note>. The phrase "All scripture is given by inspiration of God" <note>original: "πασα γραφη θεοπνευστος" (pasa graphē theopneustos)</note>, 2 Timothy 3:16, does not contradict my presentation at all; rather, it is a quite visible limitation of the Jewish canon. However, from this contemporary usage—directed against those Jews who already attributed divine inspiration to these books—it cannot be proven that this is simultaneously a binding indication or judgment for all other readers, whereby their own examination would be declared entirely dispensable and unnecessary for those who are not Jews.

One can easily be convinced of this. Jesus held a long conversation with the Samaritan woman, even concerning religion itself; it is well known that the Samaritans have only the five books of Moses <term>Pentateuch: the first five books of the Hebrew Bible</term> in their canon. Likewise, Jesus disputed with the Sadducees using only the five books of Moses. Now, just as Jesus did not once and for all confirm for these listeners that all the remaining books—which the other Jews have under the two classes of *Nebiim* <note>Hebrew: "Prophets"</note> and *Ketubim* <note>Hebrew: "Writings"</note>—were not divine books simply because the Samaritans did not regard them as such: so it is also quite correct conversely, when Jesus deals with the other Jews, who together...

<meta>catchword: ...men</meta>

<summary>The author argues that Jesus and the Apostles engaged with the Jewish canon based on the existing beliefs of their audience, rather than establishing a universal rule that bypasses individual critical examination. He uses the example of the Samaritans and Sadducees to show that Jesus adapted his scriptural arguments to the specific canon accepted by his interlocutors.</summary>

Within Paul’s letters, there are passages—as several of the older Christian writers themselves indicate—cited from certain so-called Apocrypha <note>Non-canonical books not included in the standard Bible</note>, which were attributed to certain authors from the Old Testament even before Paul’s time. Furthermore, in both the Second Letter of Peter and the Letter of Jude, a piece of the narrative is cited which appears in the book attributed to Enoch <note>The Book of Enoch, an ancient Jewish religious work</note>. These citations occur because these books already held some degree of respect among those people, and yet such readers were not capable at that time of a better insight into the spurious nature of these books; nevertheless, these passages were conducive to the final purpose of Paul, Peter, and Jude. No one, however, will conclude from this a kind of divinity that Paul, Peter, or Jude would have thereby affirmed and confirmed for other readers. I am well aware that our theologians have otherwise made it a principle: because books from the Old Testament are cited by Christ or the Apostles and applied for instruction, this constitutes a confirmation of their divine origin. However, I have never repeated this entirely uncertain proposition as an actually valid proof. The reason why **Jesus** and the Apostles cite such books from time to time, in order to prove and support their teachings among the Jews, is primarily this: because the Jews already accepted these books as the source of religious truths; and further, because such passages were useful for the purpose of **Jesus** and the Apostles at that time. The first Christians therefore brought these various Jewish canons <note>The term "canon" refers to the officially accepted list of sacred books.</note> with them from the start; some held to the Hebrew version, while others used the Greek version from Alexandria, and so on. Just as had been done previously, the teachers had to continue to adapt to their listeners and, of course, continue to use such books among them. Initially, one was primarily dealing with Jews; and for them, the main content of the new doctrine (I may call it such, for it truly is so) and the sound basis of the new religion had to be demonstrated from the books which they already accepted in this context.

To this it must be added that the first Christians were actually regarded as a sect and party of the Jews, and under this guise, they shared in the tolerance and the rights of the Jews within the Roman Empire. # On Free Inquiry

I must mention that they partly praise and partly defend such works, even though they themselves, in their own conscience, do not accept them as a universal proof. However, I cannot help it if pious, sincere readers feel unrest and torment over this; I say it straight out: for capable, reflective Christians, this is never a proof, and it was only intended to serve those Jews, in those specific circumstances, as a more accessible entry point <note>The author is likely referring to the use of "accommodation," where a teacher uses the existing (though perhaps flawed) logic of their audience to lead them toward a new truth.</note>.

Among the Rabbis, it is a well-known matter that they frequently use the method of <term>Asmakhta <note>original: "על דרך אסמכתא" (al derekh asmakhta). A rabbinic legal principle where a verse is used as a "support" or memory aid for a law, rather than its actual source.</note></term>, allegorically, <note>original: "per modum disputandi opinabilem" (Latin: by way of a debatable manner of disputing)</note>, confirming their thesis through a biblical passage for the sake of memory, not for the sake of conviction. The conviction rested either on a general ground, which they shared apart from the <term>allegation <note>The act of citing a text as proof.</note></term>, or on the authority of the teacher; the citations were only intended to serve to keep their doctrine in the memory, because the biblical passage is very similar and sounds almost the same, partly in words and partly in substance. Therefore, in the Talmud, they have so often said when they cite a passage: <note>original: "ne legas sic, sed sic" (Latin: Do not read it thus, but thus)</note>; this is not a correction of the biblical text which they cite in this way; rather, through the greater or smaller change of a word, the cited text becomes even more suitable for impressing upon the memory what they had just taught. Buxtorf <note>Johannes Buxtorf (1564–1629), a famous Hebraist and scholar of Jewish tradition.</note>

The inner welfare of such differently situated people is interrupted neither by pagan authorities nor by external changes; they have received an entirely different capability; everything must now serve for their true best. In regard to the Gentiles, however, partly the entire offense is removed which the crude opinions of the Jews necessarily created, and partly the concept of inner improvement and true participation in God’s perfections is communicated to them in such a way that they could advantageously unite it with the natural knowledge they already possessed. And so, step by step, the great ultimate purpose of God is better accomplished among the human race, provided that one always prefers the recognized better over the former worse.

# On Free Inquiry

For Jews, a detailed account is primarily given of the great miracles that Jesus had performed <note>original: "gethan"</note>, who is described in such a way that he holds precedence over Moses and all the prophets. For this reason, the written composition—which was now intended to take the place of oral instruction for other aspiring teachers—was arranged in the same manner.

Matthew did not write for Jews in Palestine, although people have gradually imagined this to be the case; nor did he write for Greek or Alexandrian Jews. Rather, he wrote for Jews in Arabia or similar foreign regions, whether it was in the Hebrew, Syrian <note>likely referring to Aramaic or Syriac</note>, or Greek language of that time.

John, on the other hand, assumes readers who, according to the circumstances God had arranged for their lives and upbringing, were already more familiar with reflecting upon truths. He has therefore recorded very little of the miracles, but more of the speeches and teachings of Jesus. Now, was it, for example, inherently and actually necessary for the readers of John’s Gospel to have Matthew’s narrative? Did Matthew really have an intention, in his description, for all people of all time? Does the personal improvement and spiritual welfare of a human being really depend on every person accepting exactly four Gospels—one just like the other—as divinely inspired writings <term>divinely inspired writings: the theological concept of "Theopneusty," where scripture is authored by God through humans</term>? I do not believe so. Just as John reports that Jesus performed many more miracles

# Of the Canon.

Historical descriptions of such sensory things—sitting at the customs booth, casting nets, crucifying someone, etc.—always carry with them the words naturally belonging to those actions. Matthew and John, therefore, did not require a divine inspiration <note>original: "göttliche Eingebung"</note> for this; they did, however, require it for their own moral transformations, for living knowledge, and for a faithful, honest disposition of mind, according to which they gladly sought to promote the divine purpose wisely and in the best possible way through their teaching and writings. This Christian, moreover, uses all truths, in and of themselves, as truths, whereby their divine origin and their identical, unceasing purpose are entirely beyond dispute; he experiences for his own benefit the divine power of these truths; and now he can well leave it undecided to what extent the concept of an **Inspiration** took place, since it is a historical matter which occurred outside of him, within those writers.

It is, in any case, as I believe, an almost entirely useless assertion of the former theologians when they extend **Inspiration** even to every single word, in the opinion that these words would thereby be much more appropriate and fruitful signs for us nowadays than if one did not assume such an **Inspiration** to be present. I do not wish to dwell on the fact that this is a theological dispute which affects the personal inner conduct of a Christian, in order to always

I merely note that I myself do not doubt that those writers were in a particular state of emotional or spiritual agitation <note>original: "Gemüthsbewegung"</note> when they wrote their essays, or rather dictated them, as this latter point is likely beyond doubt for most. This is because the copies <note>handwritten reproductions made before the printing press</note> brought so many changes with them, even in the first 4 or 5 centuries, that there are now many passages where the **different readings** <term>variantes lectiones: variations in the text found across different ancient manuscripts</term> are of such a nature that, in some cases, it cannot be generally proven at all that only this, and not that, was the original number, order, and construction of the actual words. Furthermore, from the printed text, entire lines, sentences, and individual words must very often be omitted, and the text must first be shortened again; and in other parts, some words must be exchanged for others. These three propositions or observations have, according to **criticism** <term>Critik: the scholarly method of analyzing historical texts to find the original meaning</term>, their established correctness among those with moderate knowledge; and they indeed concern **the scripture, as they call it** <note>original Latin: "scripturam, quam dicunt"</note>

# of the Canon.

Therefore, the question of **inspiration** is not nearly as significant as it is still commonly regarded to be. Suppose, for example, that in John 8 the entire story of the **adulteress** <note>The *Pericope Adulterae* (John 7:53–8:11), a passage famously missing from many early Greek manuscripts.</note> were to be omitted entirely, as it was missing in many former copies and translations of major church factions: in that case, a piece of the so-called <term>sacred scripture</term> <note>original: "scriptura sacra"</note> would be missing, but **nothing of the Word of God** would be missing at all. The Word is and remains unchangeable, despite all those accidental and constant changes to a text whose scribes certainly enjoyed no divine assistance. And truly, it would be quite ridiculous if Christians were to demand such assistance as a prerequisite for expecting useful knowledge from the **truths themselves**—insofar as they convey concepts—only after they first knew exactly which words and which order of words Paul had used?

### §. 23.

These same reflective Christians have the freedom to judge the Epistle to Philemon as Christians, without anxious or ignoble fear; if they find nothing therein that pertains to a divine life and conduct, then it is indeed dear to them because it belongs to the history of Paul <note>original: "Pauli Historie"</note>, but they do not ascribe it to a divine inspiration simply because so-called **Theologians** <note>original: "Theologi"</note> have done so, or because the short letter stands within the Canon. No **Theologian** has obtained a territory or a legislative power through his usual, however great, erudition; a Christian who is not strictly a scholar but is a thinker can possess nobler and greater resolutions than a very great scholar; he does not possess such gifts of the mind in vain; he should use them for the glory of God just as much as the **Theologian** may ever be capable of doing. He is therefore free to read this letter in a different light than the Epistle to the Romans **and so forth** <note>original: "&c."</note>. Knowledge can and should not serve as a snare to anyone, whereby he would be placed, as it were, in constant unrest and anxiety; whoever can look through into the perfect law of liberty <note>A reference to James 1:25</note>, into the much freer order for our spiritual perfection, will not be held captive by the narrower conscience of his fellow Christians.

# On Free Inquiry

If only it is true that everything you think and do is thought and done for the ever-increasing promotion of the **Glory of God**. This is the fundamental principle of the Christian religion, through which it wins over all people and gives them free opportunity to know God according to their own measure, and to utilize the teachings of Christ in the very best way. This becomes a **unity in the spirit**, despite all differences in internal capacities, just as the external differences of all people remain in their status and profession, and yet they all can and should be righteous Christians together. So it may go with the *Epistle of Jude*, with the *Second Epistle of Peter* <note>These two New Testament books were often debated regarding their apostolic authorship and place in the biblical canon</note>; and all theological, learned, and even angry responses cannot abolish or reshape the inner taste of a righteous, not unpracticed Christian; it rests on one's own perceptions and the agreement of the conscience; he honors God and thanks God from his heart for this ever-increasing and wholesome light that he experiences.

->§. 24.<-

A pleasant phenomenon has become known to me in this regard: that not a few honorable country preachers act with far more healthy moderation and more correct judgment than many scholars, who, as it were, have the express calling never to allow themselves to be easily surpassed. The unworthy conduct of Professor Schmid in Leipzig—who, in his *Library*, had undertaken to rescue the more common opinion regarding the clear divinity of the <term>Apocalypse</term> <note>The Book of Revelation</note>—I will pass over with a disposition more suited to the calling and status of an academic scholar. I am, in any case, certain that this unworthy behavior will draw the displeasure of many, indeed even most readers, who can easily perceive how little freedom in theological and Christian reflections or investigations would remain for them if a Pro-

# Concerning the Canon

Readers may now judge for themselves, in all Christian or scholarly freedom, whether I have remained in debt to Chancellor Reuß for an answer out of necessity or embarrassment, and whether his so zealous defense truly carries with it such an easy conviction that readers of both writings would nevertheless take the Chancellor's side. I act with a right joyful conscience, and will gladly continue the investigation honestly with anyone, if necessary, without needing to use anathemas <note>formal ecclesiastical curses or excommunications</note> or words of power. I now provide the Chancellor's reasons along with my answers, without knowingly committing any infidelity.

# Answer

The wisdom of God has employed many honest authors to prompt a conscientious investigation into old and fruitless theories; everyone is free to judge this according to their conscience, just as it was formerly free to follow Peter or Paul. I know that I certainly do not belong to the <term>crowd of idle troublemakers</term> <note>original: "turba maleferiatorum"</note> <term>who hiss at every divine revelation</term> <note>original: "quae omnem reuelationem diuinam exsibilat"</note>; even if I cannot view the <term>Revelation of St. John</term> <note>original: "Apocalypsin"</note>, Ruth, Esther, the Song of Solomon, and several similar writings as parts of a <term>divine revelation</term> <note>original: "diuinae reuelationis"</note>, and cannot honestly persuade others to imagine a divinity in them if they cannot easily find it there. That a much greater danger finally looms for the Church, especially from me and free seekers of truth like me: this I cannot see. It is certain that this book was formerly very rarely read, and until now has been used by no one for their own particular Christian perfection because no one understands it; the remaining ideas, however, have long been much better known to us and do not accrue to us from this book. What great danger shall now arise for the Church if I and a few scholars exercise the freedom to speak our thoughts straight out? I believe that there will always remain 50 or 100 against one, given the sluggish way of studying and teaching which is now the most common and therefore the so-called orthodox one: who all [accept] this book and all their...

->§. 2.<-

The great difficulty in understanding this book—which also occurs with other prophetic utterances—is beyond doubt; hence, the strange effort to explain it has greatly unsettled many. Many have also suffered harm because they were so bold as to determine the events that were supposed to concern the Church or the State; for this reason, some who lacked sound principles <note>original: "principia"</note> have set the value of the book quite low. It is therefore not to be blamed that some disapprove of this itch <note>A metaphorical "itch" or "tickle" (Kizel) referring to a restless curiosity or urge.</note> and the haste of such inquisitive people.

# On the Tübingen Writing

I will only add this: that the darkness and difficulty of a book, or of many passages within it—when they absolutely do not allow for an increase or improvement of knowledge, nor a promotion of moral or spiritual perfection—gives all thinking readers a right, by this very fact, to leave such books or passages unread. This is so that they do not waste precious time or corrupt the dignity and nobility of the Christian frame of mind. *He who does not wish to be understood, ought not to be read* <note>original Latin: "Qui non vult intelligi, non debet legi." A maxim often attributed to Jerome regarding the difficulty of certain texts.</note>; and one cannot excuse oneself through "mysteries" or "high matters" in cases where one cannot grasp anything reasonable in thought. Neither Christ nor the Apostles ever presented such books (such as the Song of Solomon) or such passages from the Prophets (such as Ezekiel’s Temple, etc.) to their listeners. Christians must truly have the right—without always having to consult the Church Councils and the theologians—to test for themselves whether a content is of the kind that Paul so clearly characterizes as *divinely inspired scripture* <note>original Greek: "theopneuston graphēn"</note> in 2 Timothy 3: namely, that it must be useful for instruction (in place of previous ignorance), for reproof (concerning previous practical errors), and so forth. # Answer

It is well known enough that there are those who proceed from thorough <term>principles<note>original: "Principiis"</note></term> yet are helpful to no one else, despite all their great arrogance regarding their own advantages. It is also demonstrable enough that all <term>commentaries<note>original: "Commentarii"</note></term> written thus far have failed to make this book more powerful or useful for the reader; indeed, not a few readers have been rendered almost insane and useless for the whole of human life by them. Furthermore, I have been called to a public teaching office and to the supervision of the sound establishment of the doctrine of the Christian religion, and have been sacredly obligated according to my best conscience, just as much as other professors of theology at other academies. If these others truly possess and apply more solid principles, then the honor and merit are theirs, and I must be content with my own limitations and lack of foundation; meanwhile, I marvel at the fact that the largest crowd is supposed to be in possession of these more solid principles. It will be seen what strong and valid grounds these principles have when they are brought to light.

No one can deny this, <note>original: "nisi rerum diuinarum proteruus contemtor et cauillator ineptus"</note> *unless they are a brazen scorner of divine matters and an absurd caviller.* People do not stop at saying, for instance: the historical origin and good provenance of this book is not yet as clear to us as with other books; or that it depends on each person's judgment and conscience. # On the Tübingen Writing

Some cast doubt upon parts of the revealed doctrine and seek to tear them out of the minds of men; and even the misuse of criticism <note>The author refers to "Higher Criticism," the historical-critical method of analyzing biblical texts which was emerging as a controversial academic discipline in the 18th century.</note>, the necessity of which is otherwise beyond dispute, seems to become dangerous to the dignity of some holy books.

To this I add: it is truly not unknown to me that, especially in our time—<note>original: "singularis est nostrae aetatis calamitas"</note> *the calamity of our age is unique*—godless people strive to expose the most holy religion to mockery <note>original: "ludibrio exponere"</note>. Not everything that has been brought up for a long time by theologians, including those in Tübingen, and even maintained until now as quite important and necessary, belongs to the most holy religion as actual essential parts or true sources thereof. Namely, the very common idea of the Canon <term>Canon: the official list of books recognized as Holy Scripture</term>, and the equal divine origin and value of all books and parts previously contained therein, **is not at all an essential part of the Christian religion.** One can be an upright Christian without attributing one and the same origin of divine inspiration, or the same degree thereof, to all books counted toward the Old and New Testaments, and thus also without attributing to them an equal purpose for the common good; and

# Answer

There can be no general, unchangeable certainty regarding these matters; even though there is always a general and unchangeable certainty and a real nature to the Christian religion and its actual fundamental concepts and principles. Should I, for this reason, be scolded in Tübingen as a *shameless despiser of divine things* <note>original: "proteruus contemtor rerum diuinarum"</note> and even of the entire Christian religion: then I must comfort myself with the example of Paul; he was also called a deceiver by the fanatical, ignorant zealots among the stiff-necked, harsh Jews; he was also a *despiser of divine things* <note>original: "contemtor rerum diuinarum"</note> which so many holy men, fathers, and ancestors had already established once and for all—and for which they crucified Jesus, or in other cases, had depicted Him very poorly. 

However, that which can be expressed by the phrase *to expose to ridicule* <note>original: "ludibrio exponere"</note> unfortunately happens frequently by many themselves who set themselves up as defenders of God, religion, and orthodoxy: they claim that no one else possesses religion, or walks in its divine true power, except for those who receive a certificate of approval from them; consequently, only those who believe everything they themselves believe, or imagine they believe quite correctly. There is a very great difference between the Christian religion and theology, which is presented by older or newer scholars in very unequal measure, according to varying skill and insight. This theology is subject to many changes and fluctuations,

# Answer

Some Christians do not wish to include anything in their religion for which they cannot recognize a basis within the foundational knowledge of the Holy Scripture, or for which they perceive no practical use for healing instruction and guidance toward all good works—for themselves or others—due to contradictions they believe they see. Because the knowledge of such theological propositions is free and relative, it is quite easy for reflective Christians to hold various ideas that differ from those of a conventional and <term>scholastic scholar<note>Referring to "schulgerecht," or those strictly following the formal academic traditions of the church schools.</note></term>. These differences, however, do not hinder those Christians from growing happily into the "new inner man" into which they, as Christians, are being formed. 

Just as there have been theologians who, in their honest opinion, claimed that the Holy Scripture contains propositions which must or can appear as direct contradictions to applied reason and investigative skill—yet which are still called **theological truths**—so too can it be claimed by many that in the Christian religion, there must certainly remain propositions that are not at all, or not fully, comprehensible to reason. These are customarily called <term>mysteries<note>original: "mysteria"</note></term>, and some even place the actual value of the entire Christian religion within them. This perspective is also permitted, provided that teachers actually achieve God’s honor and greater utility among mankind through this approach more effectively than they might otherwise,

<summary>This page discusses the intellectual freedom of Christians to prioritize practical biblical instruction over complex scholastic dogmas and explores the role of "mysteries" that transcend human reason.</summary>
<keywords>religious reason, Holy Scripture, theological truths, mysteries, spiritual growth, scholasticism, Christian liberty</keywords>
