I think it is not possible that I may again be called the author of a new opinion herewith; since <term>Theodoret<note>Theodoret of Cyrus (c. 393–458 AD), a theologian who opposed the extreme positions of Cyril of Alexandria</note></term> already straightaway rejected as innovations those mysteries which the Egyptian theologians—following the great arrogance of <term>Cyril<note>Cyril of Alexandria (c. 376–444 AD), known for his rigorous and sometimes forceful defense of Christological orthodoxy</note></term>—wanted to force upon the Oriental and other scholars regarding the doctrine of Christ's person and two natures. Truly, there is so much in the Christian religion (not merely left over as a necessity) of actual, truly universal practical truths, that one does not exactly find it necessary to add more to it from new authority from time to time than what the Holy Scripture reckons for all people. The rest does not belong in the Christian religion at all, but rather in theology—which is necessary for a different purpose—or the characteristic teaching of the Church regarding the difference of dogmatic ideas which public teachers have brought forward from time to time. Whether criticism becomes dangerous for the dignity of the <term>sacred books</term><note>original: "librorum sacrorum"</note>, at least for certain ones, I cannot say; for one must necessarily distinguish the designation "sacred books" or "sacred scripture" from the universally beneficial, immutable **Word of God**. Now, it is easy to recognize that the dignity which belongs to the **Word of God** certainly does not apply to all sacred books, nor to all their parts, in the same way. When I, for example, call the books of *Esther*, *Ruth*, or the *Song of Solomon* "sacred books," or include them under the historical term "sacred scripture," they nevertheless remain distinct from the **Word of God** which is contained here and there within the sacred scripture.

This is because no one can honestly, or with any public justification, transform those coarse and sometimes absurd images and depictions into doctrines and concepts of Christian holiness. # Answer

Arguments that were valid only for the Jews of that time do not possess a universal power of conviction, regardless of the era. Could it ever even be used for such a purpose? But I will let even this point pass; now let the author prove quite successfully and easily that this John was truly the creator. # On the Tübingen Writing

The students in **Tübingen** are to be cared for in this way; I must truly do the same in **Halle**, without necessarily agreeing with his opinion. However, as scholars, we must instruct the conscience thoroughly; we must not merely restrict and govern it with a stern, orthodox demeanor. I do not demand of any thinking student of theology <note>original: "Studiofo Theologiae"</note> that he should give me his approval outright; I even quite gladly allow that some may adopt a different opinion in this or that matter. Learned knowledge is free, as soon as it is distinguished from mere memory; and whoever accepts my opinion does so in the use and feeling of freedom.

->§. 8.<-

This report of far too great a wordiness, which could easily have arisen, does not exactly show that the Mr. Author had previously considered the matter itself sufficiently. It is entirely the same thing to *demonstrate the authenticity* <note>original Latin: "demonstrare authentiam"</note> of this book—which is indeed hereby demonstrated as the Apostle's book—and to *vindicate it for John the Apostle and Evangelist* <note>original Latin: "vindicare eam Iohanni Apostolo et Euangelistae"</note>; and it might well not have been necessary to make such a long-winded arrangement. However, the judgment as to whether some things are omitted here, or can be conveniently enough looked up elsewhere (it is also the same thing), is very uncertain; it will be shown that a great deal could have been omitted—thus making the treatise much smaller on that side—but on an entirely different side, it could have been composed much larger and more thoroughly. It matters little that *Students* <note>original Latin: "Studiosi"</note> take care of the memory; judgment is the most necessary thing; but this is still the flaw in the method of teaching <term>Lehrart: the systematic way a subject is presented to students</term>, that one merely challenges the memory, but, as it were, puts shackles on the understanding. Here stands an *Observation. By the Students* <note>original Latin: "Obseruatio. A Studiosis"</note>

*The theological works deserve to be read, especially the notable and learned* Leonard Twells’ defense of the Apocalypse <note>original: "vindiciae apocalypfeos"; Twells was an 18th-century English theologian who defended the authenticity of the Book of Revelation</note>; Wolf <note>Johann Christoph Wolf, a prominent German Hebraist and bibliographer</note> judged them worthy, etc. However, it has been well known for a long time that Twells provided a very shallow and not particularly learned treatise in these defenses. Following this, the *summary of Twells' demonstration* <note>original: "summa demonstrationis Twelfianae"</note> is shared and left without the slightest judgment, as if it were very well executed. This, then, is the *summary of Twells' demonstration*: *No book is estimated to be spurious <note>original: "spurius"; false or forged</note> which carries these four factors with it: 1) that it reflects the same age in which the writer whose name it bears flourished; 2) that writers who lived either during or shortly after its publication ascribed it to him; 3) that nothing exists in the book itself which opposes the undisputed writings of the same author; 4) that the style itself does not openly conflict with the manner of writing otherwise accepted for the credited author.* For, if an ancient book could nevertheless be considered inauthentic despite having all four of these characteristics, then no ancient monument could ever be proven authentic again. *Now, indeed, all these marks are found in—*

# Answer

The author says nothing more, as if all of this were now very good and thorough. However, if I am to state my opinion briefly, this is a wretched and unfit <term>demonstration; proof or logical argument</term>. When the <term>major and minor; the primary and secondary premises of a syllogism</term> are false, then things look quite poor for the <term>ergo or itaque; "therefore" or "consequently"</term>. Twells confuses the <term>negative and positive; the difference between proving what a thing is not versus what it is</term>. If a book is supposed to be ancient and the genuine writing of a certain author, and it lacks these four elements, then it is spurious; one can indeed conclude that from the elements cited. However, one cannot conclude <term>positively; with certainty of affirmation</term> that a writing which possesses these four elements is certainly genuine. <note>original: "Negatur consequentia."</note> The logical consequence is denied.

If people misused Paul's name to pass off letters as being from him—as Paul already warns in **2 Thessalonians 2:2 and 3:17**—then certainly at that time, during Paul's own era, (1) such letters existed, or they fit into that time; <note>original: "aetatem referebant"</note> they reflected the age. (2) Thus, even then or immediately afterward, many people attributed these letters to Paul; (3) they also took care not to fall into a direct contradiction with Paul's teachings and ordinances; and (4) they did not intentionally make the style of writing dissimilar. If one further considers the correspondence of Christ with <note>Abgarus V, a legendary King of Edessa who was said to have exchanged letters with Jesus</note> Abgar, and many other spurious pieces, these would all have to be genuine if one concludes positively. Even Tertullian <note>An early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155 – c. 220 AD)</note>

# Answer

In his *Demonstratio Evangelica* <note>original: "demonstratio euangelica"; a work by Eusebius of Caesarea defending Christianity</note> (Book 3), he places the banishment of **John** to Patmos at the very same time as the deaths of Peter and Paul; (so this too is a quite "certain" history; a "common consensus"?) and **Tertullian** <note>An early Christian author from Carthage</note> and the pseudo-Prochorus <note>A legendary author of a fictional biography of John</note> do the same, as does the first inventor of the fable that John was cast into boiling oil under **Nero** and afterwards banished to Patmos. This fable is based at least on a tradition of the early churches (though these may be very few. Better to say: of a few individual persons who were already fond of the Apocalypse; certainly not yet entire churches!) that John had been banished to Patmos under **Nero**. **Epiphanius** <note>A 4th-century bishop known for his writings against heresies</note> also reverses it; he places the Gospel under **Domitian**, but the Apocalypse even before **Nero**. (Is this what they call "unanimous history"?) **Arethas** <note>A 10th-century commentator on the Apocalypse</note> indeed cited the opinion of **Irenaeus**, but did not accept it; (what "agreement"!) he asserts that the Apocalypse was written before the destruction of Jerusalem, and that the older interpreters had explained the sixth seal as referring specifically to this destruction. The tradition of the Syrian Christians also agrees with this; among them, the title of the Apocalypse reads: The Revelation, which was granted to the Apostle John by God on the island of Patmos, to which he was banished by the Emperor **Nero**.

# Answer

**Eusebius** points to **Irenaeus** and **Clement of Alexandria** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD) was the first great historian of the Christian Church.</note>; compare Irenaeus II, 39 and III, 3. **Tertullian** (who is a good historian) in *On the Prescription Against Heretics*, section 36, also testifies that John was on Patmos and *wrote* the prophecy on the island. **Epiphanius** likewise testifies to this (he is a witness entirely worthy of trust—or at least he selected his information from previous sources). I will remain silent regarding **Jerome** and others; and this is with good reason, for these cannot be called witnesses, especially **Jerome**, who readily admits that the Greek churches—who ought to have known best what the actual ancient history contained—do not accept the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation</note>; and he did not exactly count **Irenaeus** and **Papias** with all their stories among the true historians.

Observation 2. John—and this is a useful observation by Grotius <note>Hugo Grotius (1583–1645), a Dutch jurist and theologian</note> regarding chapter 1, verse 9—*was deported to the island as if he were a seditious person.* <note>original: "quasi seditiosus deputatus est, in insulam"</note> This punishment and sedition, or the suspicion thereof, are connected; but I would like to see some evidence regarding John, showing that he, with his excellent doctrine and spiritual instruction, was ever regarded as seditious <term>seditiosus: someone inciting rebellion against the state</term> by any authority, especially since Paul himself was not held to be such in Rome. If he had already circulated this useless book against the Latin or Roman authorities beforehand, then such a charge would have been very natural.

# Response to the Tübingen Writing.
<margin>L 5</margin>

Observation 3. There are one or two people who translate the words in chapter 1, verse 9, *because of the word of God* <note>original: "δια τον λογον του Θεου"</note> etc., as: *for the purpose of receiving the word of God and the testimony of Christ*—suggesting that he [John] waited there to receive this divine revelation <note>original: "χρηματισμον"; a term for a formal divine communication or oracle</note>. But truly, that hypothesis rests on no historical foundation <note>original: "historico fundamento"</note>; and six hundred passages prove that the word *through/because of* <note>original: "δια"</note> in such word compositions signifies a preceding cause. This is against my view; but as far as the "historical foundation" is concerned, it is a begging of the question <note>original: "petitio principii"</note>, so to speak; for since there is no recorded history, we are investigating whether these very words might have given rise to such a fable. However, the word *through/because of* <note>original: "δια"</note> is also found in many passages according to my explanation; of this there can be no doubt at all; but whoever still doubts it may do so. It is well known that many writers <note>original: "Scriptores"</note> understood it as a preceding cause, and there was no need to cite Eusebius <note>A 4th-century Church historian</note> again; but whether they derived their idea from history alone, without using these words for that purpose: that is not found in Eusebius. I should think that the author, without needing other examples, could no longer deny this meaning after reading John 11:42: *because of the people... that they may believe* <note>original: "δια τον οχλον ινα πιστευσωσιν"</note>. Here the word *because of* <note>original: "δια"</note> stands in the meaning I have indicated, and the word *that* <note>original: "ινα"</note> explains it; therefore, *that he might bear witness concerning God* and *because of the testimony of God* are one and the same.

# On the Tübingen Writing

...partly with doctrines <note>original: "partim doctrinis"</note> (I would like to know what these are) partly with most holy precepts <note>original: "partim praeceptis sanctissimis"</note> (which we did not already know?); partly with most solemn prophecies <note>original: "partim vaticiniis grauissimis"</note>. Such beggings of the question <term>petitiones principii: a logical fallacy where the conclusion is assumed in the premise</term> should have been avoided; they are altogether too unworthy. John saw some things (actually, he writes that he saw them), heard others (he writes that he heard them), as he was in the spirit <note>original: "in spiritu"</note>; some things he received from the Lord himself, others through an angel. A strange classification! For everything is surely "in the spirit." And this is of the greatest importance <note>original: "maximi momenti"</note> (at least in Tübingen), that the book itself, and not just some parts of it, is full of the sweetest promises <note>original: "promissionum dulcissimarum"</note> (to me and other pious Christians it does not taste sweet); and is commended not only with the most effective words (is this supposed to be of the greatest importance? Would a deceiver perhaps reveal and condemn himself so clearly?), but is even fortified against violent hands by the most solemn and severe admonition in chapter 22, verses 18 and 19. The author had no need for such a solemn admonition except to give himself an air of authority among the simple-minded; just as a similarly useless oath is attached to the end of Irenaeus's <note>Saint Irenaeus, a 2nd-century theologian</note> books. No rational person would take the trouble to add even more such incomprehensible riddles; or perhaps strike some out of fear; on paper, all seven plagues, seven bowls, <term>etc.</term> <note>original: "tc."</note> did no harm. But the trumpet, if it actually let itself be heard, no man could prevent. When these alleged judgments arrive:

# Answer

Observation 2. <note>original: "Non sine certo nos et deliberato consilio..."</note> It is not without a certain and deliberate plan that we place the *first foundation of our proof* in those matters which no one could easily call into doubt—in the clear and evident testimony, I say, of the writer concerning himself and the origin of his book (Theses 1 and 2). — that is to say, the writer claims to be that specific John. I should think all of this has been well-known for a long time; for all I care, the author may call it the "first foundation of his proof," as **Cluver** <note>Likely Johannes Cluverus (1593–1633), a German theologian and historian.</note> also did; but whether other readers will also find a "first foundation of proof" for their own active intellects through this is another question. — <note>original: "credere solemus, vt fas est..."</note> we are accustomed to believe, as is right (this is a very large restriction; not much remains of "we are accustomed to believe"); the testimony of a writer concerning himself in such matters; (provided we otherwise know that he is an honest writer; but not otherwise,) when no suitable suspicion of falsehood underlies it, especially where no evidence exists of fraud committed by him. — it is absolutely necessary, in order to rightly defend the <term>authenticity</term> <gloss>original Greek: αὐθεντιαν (authentian)</gloss> of the book, that it be solidly and clearly demonstrated that the suspicion of that enormous crime and falsehood can in no way fall upon the writer of this book. # Response

*...to have shown it to be so, that it can in no way be doubted, if one considers it as the venerable author does.* <note>original Latin: "tare, se sic commonstrasse, nullo modo posse dubitari, si quis cogitet, sicut venerab. auctor."</note> This is correctly written; but that is a scholarly command which becomes ineffective as soon as it is made known.

I gladly leave it to others if they find and perceive such a *demonstration* <note>original Latin: "Demonstration"</note>—that no suspicion can exist—so easily; for my part, *I wonder at it more.* <note>original Latin: "miror magis."</note> — *For this reason*, it continues, *we have set forth those two things—which, when correctly* (much may well depend on that) *joined, have the greatest weight for the heart of the matter—a little more distinctly and fully;* <note>original Latin: "Hanc ob caussam... nos duo ista, quae recte... coniuncta maximum ad summam rei momentum habent, paulo distinctius pleniusque exposuimus;"</note> wherein this "more distinctly and fully" consists here, and how it can be done better and more extensively today than the many honest people were capable of in former times, I cannot see. *And so that it can be clearly understood that the book was not falsely attributed to the Apostle* <note>original Latin: "Utque intelligi euidenter possit, librum non esse Apostolo suppositum"</note>

# Answer

...adding also d) an *anathema* <note>original: "anathemate"; a formal curse or excommunication by the church</note>, to commend, and f) *even the universal church* <note>original: "vniuersam adeo ecclesiam"</note> (how? did it follow until then?) to deceive with an insane, most impudent lie, joined with the very crime of *blasphemy*: it is in no way credible—I add, at least in Tübingen.

It vexes me to answer such entirely insignificant things that are presented with such great importance! If the author is in total earnest that, by virtue of the content of this third thesis, it is absolutely incredible to him that an author should and would have taken it upon himself to commit such temerity, such blasphemy, and such a gruesome lie: then it should be said in the singular: I, Chancellor Reuß, cannot hold it to be credible; without making a claim for the agreement of the entire rational world to this judgment. But how clumsy is it to conclude *a priori* <note>reasoning from theoretical deduction rather than observation</note> in matters which have and retain an entirely historical nature and historical right? So many quite clear fabrications and forged writings, which bear partly the names of Moses, partly Solomon, partly Christ and the Apostles: have owed their actual existence to such a deception; one may speak of stupor, temerity, blasphemy, or lies <note>original: "stupore, temeritate, blasphemia, mendacio"</note>, whatever one may say according to the current laws of morality, where pious frauds <note>original: "piae fraudes"; the practice of using deception to promote a religious cause</note> have certainly been abolished, whatever...

# On the Tübingen Publication

One may claim what they wish. So many worthy bishops of those ancient times—indeed, entire churches—did in fact reject this book, which bore the name of John and contained such a (foolish) curse <note>original: "Anathema"; a formal ecclesiastical curse or excommunication, here referring to the warnings at the end of the Book of Revelation</note> etc. And yet, people now want to demonstrate *a priori* <note>from theoretical deduction rather than observation</note> that it is simply not possible for a person to have undertaken such a deception as those Church Fathers <note>original: "Patres"; the influential early Christian theologians</note> assumed. Namely, if someone were an honest, non-fanatical, but insightful Christian, then that would be correct; he would devise no falsehoods. But if he were a fanatical, political, and cunning theological fox, then the entire conclusion fails. What is said regarding the "whole church" is entirely useless; the *universal church* <note>original: "vniuersa ecclesia"</note> never knew or accepted this book; in the first and second centuries, one cannot speak of a *universal church* at all. On the contrary, it is quite certain that it was regarded as a deception in many churches. This actual history cannot be nullified by any dogmatic theological work.

# Answer

John suffered <note>original: "passus fuit"</note>, but he was not destined to suffer <note>original: "non passurus erat"</note> in the way some suggest. Regarding his beloved disciples and other teachers, such a conclusion—that they would not have allowed such things to happen <note>original: "non passuri erant"</note>—is equally invalid. If they were still very few in number and not always present in one place, then this false book could have been shared gradually from some "holy corner" as an important secret. Thus, the judgment "if they had allowed it" <note>original: "si passi essent"</note> carries no weight!

Now it continues: *On the contrary, the most highly proven historical monuments testify that in the times immediately following the apostles, the teachers watched with the greatest care and the highest zeal, and ensured that spurious writings—for example, Gospels, Acts, Revelations, etc.—even if they were infected by no serious or dangerous error, were not held to be true and genuine or included in the number of sacred and divine books.*

I regret that I must spend even a short time on such insignificant ideas. 

(1) Where is it true, as stated here, that "the most highly proven historical monuments testify" <note>original: "probatissima historiarum monumenta testantur"</note>? Do we perhaps have highly proven historical monuments from the first, second, or third centuries? Especially regarding the Revelation <term>Apocalypsis; the Book of Revelation</term>, how can one prove its universal acceptance in the East and West and its established divinity, when Jerome <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), translator of the Vulgate Bible</note> nevertheless says that the Greeks do not accept this book? That the Council of Laodicea <note>A regional Christian council held around 363 AD which produced a list of canonical books</note>, the Gregories <note>Likely referring to Gregory of Nazianzus and Gregory of Nyssa</note>, and others reject it? 

(2) As for the matter itself: that the leaders of the churches successfully managed with the greatest care to prevent spurious works <note>original: "ne spuria"</note> etc. from being accepted?

# Answer

The objection that many *genuine* disciples of the apostles were still surviving at that time <note>original: "diu plures adhuc genuini supererant apostolorum discipuli"</note> is easily answered. The term *genuine disciples* <note>original: "genuini discipuli"</note> contains no privilege of infallibility <note>original: "privilegium de non errando"</note> when someone presents a writing and falsely claims an apostle's name. The people of Thessalonica were also genuine disciples of Paul, and yet he considered it quite possible that letters were being issued under his name. Now, it follows that there were many disciples of the apostles; **Epiphanius** <note>A 4th-century Church Father known for his works against heresies.</note> speaks in such a way that when **Hyginus**, the Bishop in Rome, deliberated regarding **Marcion** <note>A famous 2nd-century heretic who rejected the Old Testament and much of the New Testament.</note> as to whether he should be received into the Church, several disciples of the apostles and elders <note>original: "seniores"</note> were asked for advice. (Was the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation.</note> being dealt with right here? In so far, yes; **Marcion** rejected it, and yet was he to be accepted? Therefore, it was not in the canon at that time.) Primarily, there were famous Apostolic Fathers <note>original: "Apostolici"</note>: **Clement of Rome**, **Ignatius**, **Papias**, and **Quadratus**. But does the author perhaps know of reports that these men had accepted the Apocalypse as a book of the Apostle? **Eusebius** <note>The "Father of Church History" (c. 260–339 AD).</note> speaks of even more; but this very **Eusebius** speaks so reasonably about the entire situation in which the Apocalypse finds itself, that he leaves it free to everyone to accept or reject the book; he must have found few proofs of it from the apostolic men <note>original: "viris apostolicis"</note>: so that it would have been impossible for a book to be regarded as divine by some, and yet not be accepted by others.

<summary>This page argues that the existence of direct disciples of the Apostles did not prevent the spread of forged writings or uncertainty regarding the Book of Revelation's canonical status. It cites historical figures like Eusebius and Marcion to demonstrate that the early Church was divided on the book's authenticity.</summary>

it seems to have very bad consequences for the rest of the general ecclesiastical society; at the very least, the *freedom* to which Christ has brought us is not promoted. # Answer

In a historical matter, where one relies upon others, is it necessarily always in accordance with the actual nature of the thing? Could he <note>Referring to Papias of Hierapolis, an early Christian author</note> not, therefore, have been deceived? And had Polycarp <note>Bishop of Smyrna and reputed disciple of John</note> truly told him that John the Apostle had, quite certainly, written this book himself?

Now Eusebius <note>Eusebius of Caesarea, the "Father of Church History," who expressed doubts about Papias's intellect</note>—who may judge as he wishes based on his undeniably certain insight from the time when he himself read the writings of Papias—is only now, in the 18th century, being refuted; even though no one today possesses the writings of Papias, and we truly do not have much of significance to say regarding the state of public Christian scholarship at that time, which was indeed very small.

1) Eusebius ventures <note>original: "non dubitat," meaning he does not hesitate</note> to say that Papias possessed little judgment; <note>original: "at vero, etiamfi non admodum magna ingenii vi et iudicii acumine polluisset, id certe, quem auctorem Apocal. habeat, (heist, habere dicatur,) et audire facile potuit"</note> "but truly, even if he had not been endowed with great power of mind and sharpness of judgment, he certainly could easily have heard who the author of the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation</note> is (that is, is said to be) held to be"; as if all those who merely listen, without their own examination, were not very easily deceived. Even this—whether someone was a true disciple of John, and remained so, and whether that which he claimed to know from John was in fact true, or was merely fabricated by such a person—required

today a listener of good taste. However, if Papias was without Christian taste and without judgment: then he could certainly hear one person say, "I have this from John"; and another say, "I have it from his immediate disciples," etc. <note>original: "Audire facile potuit"</note> "He could easily have heard it" is still very different from the fact expressed by <note>original: "certo audiuit"</note> "he certainly heard it"; the latter is something the author knows—or at least reports—no better or more certainly than **Eusebius**. <note>original: "et recte id percipere potuit"</note> "And he could have perceived it correctly" helps just as little; <note>original: "potuit etiam non recte percipere"</note> "he could also have not perceived it correctly." At the very least, even if **Papias**, according to his smaller and more unpracticed sensibilities, heard it correctly—<note>original: "si recte (relatiue, auf ihn selbst, und seine Uebung,) percepit id, quod alius ipsi narrabat"</note> if he correctly (relatively speaking, based on himself and his own experience) perceived that which another told him—it still does not follow that <term>ergo</term> <note>Latin: therefore</note> it is also true that John wrote and distributed this book, simply because someone else had told him so. <note>original: "Et attestari inde etiam firmiter potuit"</note> "And he could therefore also firmly attest to it"; here again I have no objection; but for me it does not carry much weight: Papias <note>original: "firmiter attestatus fuit, licet potuerit"</note> firmly attested to it, although he was able to do so; yet for **Eusebius** and many other worthy men, who knew the simple-minded Papias from his mythological fantasies, it also counted for nothing.

2) Is **Eusebius** also supposed to have acted with bias against Papias by venturing to contradict **Irenaeus**, who reports that Papias was a listener of John? This is indeed strange, and quite a peculiar way to act.

# Answer

Eusebius knew well enough that Irenaeus had said such things; however, he demonstrates that Papias, in his own preface—where he specifically spoke about himself—mentions nothing at all about having heard or seen any of the Apostles (even though he recounts from whom he collected his materials). Now, in our 18th century, Eusebius is attacked in this manner: even if it was not in the preface, could Irenaeus not have seen it in other passages written by Papias? What should one say to such proposed questions? I should think that Eusebius’s direct contradiction would be a clear answer: no, Papias himself said this nowhere; not even in the preface where he speaks of himself; much less in the book itself where he recounts the sayings of Christ, etc. Furthermore: could Irenaeus not have learned it with total certainty *from the testimonies of other trustworthy men* <note>original: "ex aliorum fidenignorum testimoniis"</note> regarding Papias? How strange is this question! Where then were these *trustworthy witnesses* <note>original: "testes fide digni"</note> at that time? There were indeed many people in Asia who had associated directly with Papias, and from these, Irenaeus supposedly heard something quite correctly that Papias himself does not report about himself! I wonder even more at the following: *certainly, the credibility of Irenaeus in this matter ought to be greater than that of Eusebius, for many reasons.* <note>original: "maior certe hac in re Irenaei fides esse debet; multas ob caussas, quam Eusebii."</note> If the author judges in earnest in this way, then more learned people will wonder that a Professor of Theology judges so, who the schol-

# Answer

Since these conjectures and reports from Tübingen are without real foundation, I must further report that Papias explicitly says so himself: he was a student or <term>listener<note>original Greek: ακροατης (akroatēs)</note></term> (not of any Apostle, but) of Aristion and of that John who is called the **Presbyter** <note>The term "Presbyter" usually refers to an elder or priest in the early church, distinguished here from John the Apostle.</note>. Now I ask the readers to judge for themselves: Papias names Aristion and John the Presbyter here in the preface as his teachers; but if he had even heard some of the Apostles in the past, why does he not also let their names be read here, in this place? Should he not rather have considered it an honor to name Apostles as well? The learned Abbot Ceillier has therefore, with complete justification, agreed with Eusebius <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD) was a historian of the early Christian Church.</note> in his *General History of Sacred Authors*, volume 1, pages 683 and 684, in note b); he also very effectively removed the objection that one might raise: namely, that Irenaeus says in Book 3, Chapter 33, that Papias was a listener of John and a companion of Polycarp. Ceillier knew that this was not our John the Apostle; he also says that Irenaeus does not mean the Apostle (as the Lord Chancellor <note>Likely referring to a specific academic or ecclesiastical official involved in the contemporary debate.</note> mistakenly believes), but rather this John the Presbyter, because otherwise Irenaeus—whenever the name John is intended to indicate the Evangelist—always adds the phrase: **the disciple of the Lord.** Ceillier also refutes the argument, should one wish to object based on Jerome's 48th letter to Theodore, that

For I considered that the instruction I could take from books would not be as advantageous for me as what I could hear orally from those who are still living. This very point is already a poor sign of Papias’s scholarship and sincerity. Where then were such books from which he could take this? He himself wanted to write a book now, because there were as yet no such books or narratives. # Response to the Tübingen Writing

<margin>N 5</margin>

<note>The author begins by quoting and refuting a Latin argument regarding the church historian Eusebius.</note>
"However, Eusebius *does not say* that Papias derived his Millenarianism <term>Chiliasm; the belief in a literal thousand-year reign of Christ on earth</term> from anywhere else but from unwritten tradition; nor could he have said so at all, if the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation</note> had been written by John the Elder, the disciple of Christ (ibid. chapter 39)."

I do not know what this is supposed to mean or how it helps; the phrases "cannot be," "could have been," and so on, find much favor with the Mister Author. I confess that, regarding the history in which we are currently engaged, I do not involve myself in such "could" and "could not" arguments. History asks about "is" and "is not," not at all about "could" and "could not." For our imagination does not create history. 

"Unwritten" <note>original: ἄγραφος (agraphos)</note> refers solely, as is clear, to the undeniable, universally accepted scriptures upon which the Christian religion rests. Now, even if Millenarianism is written in an Apocalypse which has a different John as its author than the Evangelist, it is still true and correctly stated that such a theory cannot be derived from those authentic, universally accepted scriptures. It is therefore called an "unwritten tradition" <note>original: ἄγραφος παράδοσις (agraphos paradosis)</note>, since the history concerning a "John the Elder" is a mere tradition <note>original: παράδοσις (paradosis)</note>, and consequently the content of this book, concerning the thousand-year kingdom, is likewise an unwritten tradition. What is the meaning then of "he does not say, nor could he have said"? Eusebius did indeed write and say it with all the necessary Greek letters! Such simple-minded things as Papias and Irenaeus and...

# Answer

Even if these were tools for a minor final purpose, we still must not view them as the fruits of a <term>talent</term><note>original: "ingenii"</note> that is now called "most excellent"; or as evidence of <term>learning</term><note>original: "eruditio"</note> that is now called "most extensive," or as the fruits of a "most ardent zeal for truth!" It helps even less to say: <note>original: "potuit satis superque cognitum habere..."</note> "he could have had more than enough knowledge, both as to who was considered the author of the Apocalypse, and what its authority was in those first times of the church." **Justin** <note>Justin Martyr, a 2nd-century Christian apologist</note> could have known this. But he also could *not* have known it. He could have known that there were many or few admirers of the Apocalypse; but the fact that John the Evangelist was truly the author, and that there was therefore a proper reason to deem this book worthy of respect: he himself did not know this for certain simply because others said it and he learned to repeat it after them. I do not need to further answer the passage from the *Dialogue* <note>Justin's "Dialogue with Trypho"</note>; Mr. **Strorh** <note>likely referring to Gottlob Christian Storr, a contemporary theologian</note> has sufficiently explained it; hardly anyone will build their own conviction upon such meager grounds.

->§. 18.<-

Now we come to **Irenaeus**, whose testimony is called "most weighty and most worthy of all credit"; he was born around the year 120, became Bishop of **Lyon** in 167, and held great authority in the church partly "on account of his clearly singular and exceptional learning" and partly "on account of his distinguished merits."

# On the Tübingen Writing

received. Regarding this introduction, I know of no scholar who has attributed **singular and exceptional erudition** <note>original Latin: "eruditionem singularem atque eximiam"</note> to **Irenaeus**, and anyone who has read his wretched writings certainly finds no signs of learning, let alone great—especially exceptionally great—learning. Of what use, then, are such judgments from Tübingen? Who, after all, were **Irenaeus's** teachers? **Papias**: from him **Irenaeus** learned *the sentiments of the Millenarians* <note>original French: "les sentimens de Millenaires"; referring to the belief in a literal thousand-year earthly reign of Christ</note>; **Polycarp**, who taught him piety; where else could he have acquired learning? **Tertullian** may call him an *extremely curious explorer of all doctrines* <note>original Latin: "omnium doctrinarum curiosissimus explorator"</note>, but we must not impart such a concept of the *exceptional erudition of Irenaeus* to our own times; otherwise, we would lack all words to describe our modern scholarship even in the slightest degree. Furthermore, it is not the case that **Irenaeus** was born as early as the year 120 and became Bishop in 167; **Pothinus** <note>The first bishop of Lyon and Irenaeus's predecessor</note> had only died in 177; thus, **Irenaeus** could not have become his successor ten years prior. The authors of the *Literary History of France* <note>original French: "Histoire litteraire de France"</note> have explained this more effectively and express themselves quite cautiously regarding the **learning** of this bishop.

It is now claimed: He was [knowledgeable] *of matters in the church* <note>original Latin: "rerum in ecclesia"</note> (but where was this *church*, the history of which **Irenaeus** is supposed to know so well? Was **Irenaeus** so well known in all parts of **Asia**, **Europe**, etc.?)

# Answer

<margin>Irenaeus?</margin> *...from the age of the apostles onward, he was so knowledgeable that he asserted he could enumerate those who were appointed as bishops and their successors down to his own time.* <note>original Latin: "inde ab apostolorum aetate ita gnarus, vt enumerare se posse asserat eos, qui instituti sint epi [episcopi] et successores eorum, ad sua vsque tempora."</note> Is this supposed to prove singular erudition? Since the establishment of distinct Christian societies spread gradually from one city to another, it was actually quite insignificant to count this as a special merit—this knowledge of individual bishops, who in many places perhaps totaled only three, just as in Lyon there had been only one before him. Firstly, this is no *knowledge of matters conducted within the church* <note>original Latin: "Kentnis rerum in ecclesia gestarum"</note>, and secondly, it is no proof of scholarship. Furthermore: he was a pupil of Polycarp—namely, regarding the fear of God; but had this teacher imparted a singular erudition which he himself did not possess? And Polycarp was a pupil of John? Namely? What follows from that? Did he know something from Polycarp regarding the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation</note> about which he is so uncertain that, regarding its timing, Irenaeus's opinion was abandoned shortly thereafter? Further: *he says the Apocalypse was seen almost in his own age* <note>original Latin: "dicit sua fere aetate esse visam apocalypsin"</note>; this is supposed to make his testimony all the more credible. But even Eusebius, Epiphanius, and several others abandon Irenaeus's claim as false; should we nevertheless believe him? It is quite obviously written incorrectly by this author: <gloss>almost in our own generation</gloss> <note>original Greek: "σχεδον ἐπι της ἡμετερας γενεας"</note>. This is a <term>begging of the question</term> <note>original Latin: "petitio principii"; a logical fallacy where the conclusion is assumed in the premise.</note>; the true age of Irenaeus is meant to be determined from this very passage.

# On the Tübingen Writing

The author says here of the **Apocalypse** <note>The Book of Revelation</note> that it was seen under **Domitian** <note>Roman Emperor, r. 81–96 AD</note>; now he goes on to say, "almost in my or our own time." What does this mean? **Irenaeus** <note>Bishop of Lyon and early Christian theologian, c. 130–202 AD</note> says of himself: that he was already a student of **Polycarp** in his childhood or youth; the latter, however, died at a very advanced age. The death of **Polycarp** is placed in the year 166; consequently, **Irenaeus** himself must have still been very young at that time. Assuming he was 30 years old as a presbyter, he was born around 130. **Domitian** is placed between 80 and 90; according to this, the **Apocalypse** would have been written only some 30 years before the birth of **Irenaeus**. I believe that the author quite diligently provides this determination of time himself, and does so first, so that the **Apocalypse** should find all the more credit. Perhaps, as I have already reported, the problem can be solved bit by bit as to why many passages of **Tertullian** <note>Early Christian author from Carthage</note> agree with the words of **Irenaeus**, and likewise **Clement of Alexandria**—otherwise no one but these two—use the writings of **Irenaeus**. This very assurance from **Irenaeus**: that the number in **Apocalypse** 13:18 <note>The "Number of the Beast," 666</note> stands as such in all accurate and old copies, fits poorly with that report. Did **Irenaeus** know, for example, that there were only 6 to 10 good copies, because he says "in all good copies"? Were there also younger copies which were supposedly not faithful? This seems to imply, as it were, a single diocese and a small territory,

# Answer

*Is it credible that this book was not continuously shared by these seven churches with others as well?* <note>original Latin: "credibile, quam ab his septem ecclesiis cum aliis etiam continuo communicatum eum fuisse?"</note> Regarding these sentences, what is said here is a *begging of the question* <note>original Latin: "ipsa petitio principii"; a logical fallacy where the conclusion is assumed in the premise.</note>. If it is a forged book, it would be exactly the author's intention—as the most direct means—to feign an acquaintance in Asia in order to gain entry all the more easily in Alexandria, etc. Therefore, the deceiver takes care not to mention any person by a *proper name* <note>original Latin: "nomine proprio"</note>. Consequently, the Lord Chancellor must not conclude in this way: that this book must have been sent to Sardis, and indeed by John himself. If John had written it honestly in this manner, then we must think he would certainly have sent it to these seven churches. But now everything remains hypothetical, and no history has been made out of it through this "it ought to be" and "it is necessary." Precisely because this should have been found, of course, but was not found upon inquiry, so many learned Fathers rejected the entire book. Now follows further on page 198: *Either we must believe—which all history testifies to be most foreign to all truth—that not only Melito but also those others, Irenaeus, Justin, Papias, and other leading men in the church, were either extremely senseless and cowardly men, or impostors; or we must admit that they were—plainly certain—regarding the authenticity of the book and the consensus of those churches.* <note>original Latin: "aut credere nos oportet... plane certos."</note> I do not like the more extensive descrip-

# On the Tübingen Writing

I will not transcribe the description of this certainty again; nothing further is made certain by it other than the fact that the Lord Chancellor thinks so in all seriousness—which no one likely doubts. However, regarding the **conclusion itself**: first of all, even if it retained all its force, nothing more would be produced by it than this: "Up to now, we are in this position regarding our ideas, that we must prefer one or the other, specifically the latter." But our conclusion did not actually cause John to have truly written this in the past. A brighter light may appear again, according to which we would then say ourselves: with that conclusion, we had not hit upon the truth. And in fact, events, accidental changes, and the actions of men are not to be brought out through logical conclusions when one is in a state of historical uncertainty. 

However, I have more to point out regarding both parts: 

1) That these names are called <term>principes in ecclesia viri</term> <note>original: "leading men in the church"</note> is not straightforwardly true; the many gross deficiencies of **Irenaeus**, **Justin**, and **Papias** <note>Early Christian Church Fathers from the 2nd century</note> are visible enough. As long as there are no better authors, of course, they are called at that time not only <term>principes</term> <note>leaders</note> but also the only writers from whom anything has come down to us. 

2) Even if I do not immediately call them <term>maxime vecordes et ignaros</term> <note>original: "extremely senseless and ignorant"</note>, they nevertheless remain <term>imperiti scriptores</term> <note>original: "unskilled writers"</note>. 

3) <term>Impostores</term> <note>impostors</note> (especially well-meaning ones) is not so impossible; <term>piae fraudes</term> <note>original: "pious frauds" — the practice of using deception to promote a religious cause</note> are unfortunately very old.

# Answer

If **Irenaeus** <note>Irenaeus of Lyons (c. 130–202 AD), an influential early Christian theologian and bishop.</note> already held this book in high regard himself; if it is perhaps even a deliberate work: then the <term>deception</term> <note>original: "impostura"</note> was all the easier in order to ensure this book gained acceptance. Regarding the other point: <term>there is yet a third option</term> <note>original: "datur adhuc tertium"</note>; one must indeed admit that these men remained convinced of it; but it does not follow that their conviction regarding this book must necessarily result in our own conviction. 

As for the <term>consensus of the churches</term> <note>original: "consensum ecclesiarum"</note> in the 2nd century, it may well be of very little significance. In Lyon, there was only a single bishop before **Irenaeus**; the same was true in many other cities as well—at most the 2nd or 3rd up to that point. Since the subsequent Greek churches did not accept this book, one sees that what is claimed regarding the <term>consensus of the churches</term> in the 2nd century is a mere imagination. At the very least, history is not there to support it; if someone wishes to use such modern conclusions to supplement church history, he may do so; I do not; nor is such a thing tolerated in other forms of political history. 

In the note on page 199, a passage from **Irenaeus** is cited as proof that every diligence was employed to bring out the truth <term>in doubtful and controversial matters</term> <note>original: "in rebus dubiis et controuersis"</note>; however, on one hand, this does not prove that these <term>overseers</term> <note>original: "antistites," referring to bishops or church leaders</note> actually brought out the truth, but only reveals to us what they ultimately took to be the truth;

->§. 20.<-

**Tertullian** <note>Quintus Septimius Florens Tertullianus (c. 155 – c. 220 AD), a prolific early Christian author from Carthage.</note> follows; a distinguished teacher endowed with excellent gifts (though perhaps not quite with <term>charismata <note>original: "χαρισμασι" - spiritual gifts or supernatural graces.</note></term> in the common sense?); even if this were as certain as it is not: it proves nothing in a matter which every capable Christian must investigate for himself. He has likely turned clear untruths into narratives before; if he had even the slightest appearance of a reason to suspect something, he affirms it in the form of history. Nothing can be built upon such tradition, where one person simply repeats what another says; and I do not blame the **Marcionites** <note>Followers of Marcion of Sinope, who rejected the Old Testament and parts of the New Testament, emphasizing a radical distinction between the God of the Law and the God of the Gospel.</note> and other learned Christians at all for denying the succession; and saying: we ourselves have understanding and the power of judgment which we must apply, according to our conscience, to the glory of God.

# On the Tübingen Writing

Even if one says immediately that the opponents of the Revelation <note>The biblical Book of Revelation or Apocalypse</note>—who existed from the very moment it became known—possess no authority, I was still quite right to write that this book met with contradiction from its earliest days. # Answer

**Irenaeus, Justin, and Tertullian** <note>Irenaeus of Lyons (c. 130–202 AD), Justin Martyr (c. 100–165 AD), and Tertullian (c. 155–220 AD) were influential early Christian theologians often called the "Church Fathers."</note> accepted it themselves as a divinely beneficial book, originating from the Apostle. Now the question is: (1) which teachers held authority at that time? Both of them did, within their respective parties. There is certainly no doubt about that. (2) To which teachers do we grant more authority now? This depends on the specific knowledge that one has gained, partly from church history and partly from the contents of the writings of **Irenaeus** and **Justin**. I thought that I had explained myself so clearly and fairly on this matter that even ordinary Christians, who are not entirely incapable, could help judge it. Whoever gathers church history in such a way that they accept the one-sided accusations from **Irenaeus** as entirely accurate historical descriptions will, of course, have very bad thoughts about **Marcion** and other **Gnostics** <note>Marcion (c. 85–160 AD) was a prominent early Christian figure later declared a heretic for rejecting the Old Testament; Gnostics were various groups emphasizing secret spiritual knowledge.</note>. I have long repeated what **Richard Simon** <note>A 17th-century French priest and pioneer of modern biblical criticism.</note> had already suggested: that of all the alleged falsifications of which **Tertullian** and **Epiphanius** <note>Epiphanius of Salamis (c. 310–403 AD), known for his fierce attacks on heresies.</note> accuse **Marcion** (**Irenaeus** and **Justin** do not get involved in this)—namely, that he falsified the letters of Paul—not a single such falsified passage is actually to be found in reality. Why then should one believe other things attributed to them by such poor writers who possessed so little personal insight? I may surely call them poor writers, who [possess] so little

# Answer

specifically name those who had seen this themselves and experienced it firsthand. Paul sent his letters to a specific society or church, and through these—or their students, or those who claimed to be such—these books were gradually made more widely known. Regarding the Gospels, however, it cannot even be said that this or that one was first delivered to this or that specific church; because there are no specific reports concerning this. I do not believe, therefore, that there is an inherited and propagated report stating that **Matthew**, **Luke**, etc., delivered their books directly to this or that teacher. It was not known. 

2) In general, there is no history at all from the first two centuries that would allow one to say that the reports from the 4th century deviate from the older ones and are therefore to be rejected as more recent. The history of the Christians is, as it were, a secret history during these times; even Papias <note>Papias of Hierapolis (c. 60–130 AD), an early Christian author whose works are mostly lost.</note> did not inquire much after the books which, by virtue of such testimonies, were said to have been written by the Apostles; instead, he explicitly preferred inquiring after the oral accounts that were carried around from the Apostles. It is quite exceptionally strange, when we compare our own thoughts against it, that he writes: "For I did not suppose that information from books <note>original: "οὐ γὰρ τὰ ἐκ τῶν βιβλίων"</note> (namely Gospels, etc.) would help me so much as information from a living and abiding voice." <note>original: "ὅσον τὰ παρὰ ζώσης φωνῆς καὶ μενούσης." This famous quote from Papias suggests he valued oral tradition over written texts.</note>

Regarding the remark: *if they allow themselves to be moved by theological arguments to reject a book, then they cease to be witnesses:* I think it is necessary to distinguish what I have already noted further back. History and testimonies of events in the external, visible world are of a different kind than these statements or reports, which are called "testimonies" here and are entirely distinguished from one's own judgments. One can be an **eyewitness** to the fact that a man born blind was then made to see; this is an event in the external world. One can watch or listen when an **Apostle** writes or dictates something; and such external events truly allow for such testimonies. Now, I have already said: there are no witnesses at all—at least I know of not a single one from the entire ancient **Church History** <note>original: "Kirchenhistorie"</note> to be named or otherwise described—who were present and watched as **John** wrote this **Revelation**, or when he handed this book over to this or that teacher. Not a single person can be named who says: "It was I," or "I was there," when John distributed this written book. This would certainly be the kind of history where there could be witnesses; but we have no such witnesses. Since now only indirect witnesses come forward, and even those without names,

# Response

those who circulate this Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation</note> and claim they were informed by others—who certainly should have known the truth—that this book originates from John: it is clear then that one must not say everything depends solely on such witnesses. It depends just as much on the **moral character** of these witnesses as it does on the **content** of the book, specifically whether it is of such a nature that it can truly be attributed to someone whose system of thought is already known. The same applies to the following: "And indeed, if the Bible is to be the source of knowledge for theology, then it is a bit backwards to determine beforehand what constitutes theological error, and then try to examine the books based on that to see if they are canonical <note>Accepted as authoritative scripture</note> or apocryphal <note>Of doubtful authenticity or outside the official canon</note>." I would offer the following explanation here, which I consider to be quite properly founded.

1. The Bible, plainly speaking as a collection of all the books that are now customarily written or printed together, is not—strictly speaking—what one calls the ground of knowledge for theology. In many of these books, there is nothing contained at all that could belong to Christian theology; no one cites theological proofs from **Esther, Ruth, Ezra, Nehemiah**, or from the histories of **Samson, Abimelech, Jeroboam**, etc.

2. If the correct concept is applied, namely **God's Word**, or the more detailed instruction for the true and greater improvement of mankind: then this is indeed

# On the Tübingen Writing

<column-break/>

[The oral tradition] existed long before there were written books, which contain the summary or the repetition of oral instruction and were intended to pass it on to others. The first Christians were therefore frequently reminded, even by the Apostles, to "test the spirits" <note>A reference to 1 John 4:1: "Beloved, do not believe every spirit, but test the spirits to see whether they are from God."</note>—namely, according to the principles already obtained from oral instruction; for at that time, the very first beginnings were being made in composing and distributing written essays. 

3) From some such writings—the content of which offers no offense to a reader who already knows moral concepts and truths and has begun to experience them—the judgment could quite rightly always be derived: whether one could indeed accept this or that other writing, which was now to be accepted as divine, as such? If the content does not carry this [divine quality] with it, then the reader follows his own principles and does not concern himself with a large or small multitude of other people who judge differently. 

4) One cannot even act otherwise with many books, because it is impossible to reach a clear conclusion through external historical investigation. It may be that the so-called witnesses on both sides carry equal weight—as was the case, namely, with the *Apocalypse* <note>original: "apocalypsis"; referring to the Book of Revelation, which faced significant debate regarding its inclusion in the biblical canon</note> even in the earliest times. For this reason, **Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD), a historian of the early Church who categorized New Testament books into those accepted, disputed, or rejected</note> leaves it free for all teachers (for it was likely for them that he primarily wrote) to do as they wish; or it may be that ancient reports no longer exist...

# Answer

...that they confirmed it to be so: *if they had known that doubtful opinions about it had existed among their predecessors?* <note>original Latin: "si dubias de ea exstitisse sententias antecessorum cognovissent?"</note> This I cannot understand. If they had become acquainted with such doubtful opinions, they would have had to strive all the more to make their own ideas and judgment prevail! And I believe this intention is clear enough in **Irenaeus** <note>Bishop of Lyon (c. 130–202 AD), an early Christian theologian known for defending orthodoxy.</note>. Furthermore, if these very witnesses adopted such coarse Jewish content—which led the later teachers **Jerome, Augustine, and Gelasius** to publicly reject this method of teaching and interpretation, even naming the teachers **Irenaeus, Tertullian, Lactantius, and Victorinus**—then it is as clear as day: these witnesses cannot deserve our approval on their own merit. Rather, our own investigation, based on our much better principles, must first judge their entire way of thinking.

# On the Tübingen Writing

**Tertullian**, **Lactantius**, and **Victorinus of Pettau** <note>Victorinus of Pettau (died 303/304) was an early Christian exegete and the first known Latin commentator on the Book of Revelation.</note> are among the authors who adopted "Jewish fables" <note>original: "iudaicas fabulas"</note> from the Apocalypse; it is also well known that his writings were rejected as apocryphal by **Gelasius** <note>Pope Gelasius I (reigned 492–496), associated with the 'Decretum Gelasianum' which listed canonical and apocryphal books.</note>. Does such an author truly belong among the witnesses for the divinity of this book? **Methodius** certainly cites the Apocalypse as a book that John wrote through the inspiration of Christ (in his *Symposium* <note>original: "συμποσίῳ" - referring to Methodius of Olympus's work 'The Banquet of the Ten Virgins'.</note>); however, in this very same book, he also attributes the Book of Wisdom to the inspiration of the Holy Spirit. Nothing more is needed to show that such a witness must likewise first be subjected to our critical judgment. **Pamphilus** is mentioned next; but since **Eusebius** already states that he wrote nothing except a few letters, perhaps to his friends, and **Jerome** had read nothing by him, I do not know what information one might have that Pamphilus himself had accepted the Apocalypse—especially since other learned presbyters did not accept it. If, against these insignificant witnesses, we weigh the **Council of Laodicea** <note>A regional synod of approximately thirty clergymen from Asia Minor assembled about 363–364 AD.</note>, which consisted of 22 bishops from Asia Minor, and which in its final canon publicly named the books intended for church use and omitted the Apocalypse: then surely the authority of 22 bishops in their own province carries more weight than such passages from individual authors. Indeed, it was in Asia Minor itself that this

# Answer

The book must have been accepted by the bishops of the individual cities from the very beginning, if it had ever been historically true that John had sent and delivered such letters as appear therein to these congregations; even though the Lord Chancellor <note>Likely referring to Johann David Michaelis, a prominent 18th-century theologian</note> opined, *it was necessary* <note>original: "necesse fuit"</note>, that these letters be sent around to the seven congregations. If it is not an Egyptian fable: then it is right to conclude so; for if John wants to tell these churches what he has to say: then he would not have hidden it behind his stove <note>An idiom meaning to keep something secret or tucked away</note>.

->§. 26.<-

Coming to Caius and Dionysius; I will not dwell here at all, as this investigation has been conducted most clearly in Mr. Stroth's treatise. I only note further: Dionysius expressly says that some, even before his time, not only rejected this book, but also refuted it or judged its content to be unchristian and false. Even if we do not know who these people are who expressly refuted this book according to its entire content: it is nevertheless a certain indication that we read here; which truly carries more weight than the fact that simple, fanatical people, like Irenaeus, Justin, and others in greater numbers, accepted it. The public rejection at the Council of Laodicea <note>original: "concilio laodiceno"; a 4th-century synod that produced a list of canonical books excluding Revelation</note> becomes more comprehensible through this. ->§. 27.<-

I shall now explain the passage from Caius <note>A 3rd-century Roman presbyter known for opposing the Book of Revelation</note>. After Caius had said: Cerinthus, <note>original: "ὁ δι’ ἀποκαλύψεων, ὡς ὑπὸ Ἀποστόλου μεγάλου γεγραμμένων, τερατολογίας ἡμῖν, ὡς δι’ ἀγγέλων αὐτῷ δεδειγμένας ψευδόμενος ἐπεισάγει λέγων"</note> "who by means of revelations, as if written by a great Apostle, falsely introduces to us monstrous tales as if they were shown to him by angels," claiming that the kingdom of Christ would be an earthly one after the resurrection, and that men in the body would enjoy pleasures and lust in Jerusalem, just as they do now—<note>original: "πάλιν ἐπιθυμίαις καὶ ἡδοναῖς ἐν Ἱερουσαλὴμ τὴν σάρκα, πολιτευομένην, δουλεύειν"</note> "the flesh, living in Jerusalem, would again be a slave to desires and pleasures"—Caius then continues: <note>original: "καὶ ἐχθρὸς ὑπάρχων ταῖς γραφαῖς τοῦ Θεοῦ, ἀριθμὸν χιλιονταετίας, ἐν γάμῳ ἑορτῆς, θέλων πλανᾷν, λέγει γίνεσθαι"</note> "and being an enemy to the divine Scriptures, wishing to deceive, he says there will be a period of a thousand years spent in wedding festivities."

The Lord Chancellor <note>Referring to Christoph Matthäus Pfaff of Tübingen</note> now wishes to explain this last part on page 223 in this way: *He says that he [Cerinthus] was an enemy of the divine Scriptures, and that he distorted that millennium preached in those sacred writings—(but where indeed, the Chancellor adds so easily and confidently, if not in the one Apocalypse of John?)—from its true sense to his own most perverse opinions; and taught that the faithful, called back to life, would again lead an earthly life in Jerusalem—and that they would give themselves over to the most foul...*

# Answer

...in order to gain authority for those doctrines of his through revelations, as if written by a great apostle, he wished to persuade men of his monstrous ideas—as if they had been shown to him by an angel. This, I believe, is the meaning of Caius's words: that he even abused the Holy Scriptures themselves, and especially the words of the Apostle John concerning the future millennium <note>original: "millenio"; the belief in a thousand-year reign of Christ on earth</note>, but that he perverted them. On page 224, it certainly cannot be clearly proven from these words of Caius that he held Cerinthus, rather than John, to be the author of our Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation</note>, etc. 

That this is a true and demonstrable explanation of Caius's words can probably only be imagined by a reader who wants to keep the Apocalypse at all costs, even at the great expense of truth and honesty. I will therefore attempt to show my readers that this explanation of Caius's words presented here is entirely false and to completely overthrow it. The main point depends on the last clause: *and being an enemy to the Scriptures* <note>original Greek: "καὶ ἐχθρὸς ὑπάρχων ταῖς γραφαῖς"</note>. However, one must 1) not put Caius's clauses into a completely different order, as the Lord Chancellor <note>Likely referring to Johann Salomo Semler, a prominent 18th-century theologian often called the "Chancellor"</note> does, reversing his passage as if Caius began his narrative with these words, *and being an enemy to the Scriptures*, and as if everything else followed this clause; which is so obviously false. After Caius had recounted those things which are so visibly identical to the content of the Apocalypse regarding the angels and the Apostle John, he then reveals the true source...

# On the Tübingen Writing

The source of Cerinthus's <note>Cerinthus was an early Gnostic heretic who lived around 100 AD; he was often accused by later church fathers of forging the Book of Revelation to support his own views.</note> opinion, and consequently also of such a fabrication concerning angels, etc., from which his opinion was supposed to gain an appearance of truth. Gaius <note>Gaius of Rome was a 3rd-century churchman known for opposing the Book of Revelation, claiming it was written by the heretic Cerinthus.</note> therefore begins precisely with the first chapter of this Apocalypse and reports: a) Cerinthus fabricated a great Apostle and wrote revelations in his name; b) in which foolish, miraculous things are recounted; c) these were supposedly shown to him by several angels, <note>original: "δεδειγμενας" (dedeigmenas) — things having been shown.</note>. This, however, is the very word that appears so often: "I will show you" <note>original: "δειξω σοι" (deixō soi)</note>, and now he sees; "come and see"; and "I saw"; one angel after another always appears. After the stories of the angels are finished, d) <note>original: "ἐπεσαγε, λεγων" (epesage, legōn) — he introduced, saying.</note> Cerinthus further fabricates in the sequence and series of his revelation that the kingdom of Christ <note>original: "βασιλειον τȣ Χρισȣ" (basileion tou Christou)</note> is to be "earthly" <note>original: "ἐπιγειον εἶναι το" (epigeion einai to) — one must pay attention to the definite article here.</note>. Gaius, as a Christian teacher, admits that the Kingdom of Christ was described in "the scriptures of God" <note>original: "ταις γραφαις τȣ Θεȣ" (tais graphais tou Theou)</note>; but that this Kingdom of Christ would be "earthly" <note>original: "ἐπιγειος" (epigeios)</note> and on earth, he calls a dissolute fable; e) this kingdom would take place in Jerusalem; which clearly begins from the 20th chapter. Now Gaius continues: "and being an enemy" <note>original: "και ἐχθρος ὑπαρχων" (kai echthros hyparchōn)</note>. This means, as all readers who are not already biased will admit to me: since he is therefore—as one sees from Cerinthus's fabrications—an enemy of the true divine scriptures, in which the right Kingdom of Christ is described quite differently,

# Answer

This argument of the <term>tota ecclesia<note>Latin: "the whole church"</note></term> will, by and large, no longer be used so confidently or as an unprovable (indeed, entirely false) point among Protestants. It is far too shallow to immediately cite the "entire church"; and for upright Protestants, it proves nothing at all, even if the word "church" contained something more grand and worthy, and if "entire" were not so completely lacking in seriousness and so uncertain. Gregory of Nazianzus <note>A 4th-century Archbishop of Constantinople and one of the "Cappadocian Fathers"</note> called the much larger <term>Concilia<note>Latin: "Councils"</note></term> of his time a "gathering of geese and cranes," among whom he was ashamed to appear any longer. In my <term>commentariis de statu antiquo christianorum<note>Latin: "Commentaries on the ancient state of Christians"</note></term>, I have described the character of most bishops and <term>Clericorum<note>Latin: "Clergy"</note></term> somewhat more clearly than is usually implied by the sounding of the name "church." How much longer shall such lowly expedients—which would be quite fitting for Jewish Rabbis in a pinch—be carried on so extensively among us? Mr. Stroth has said more regarding Dionysius, which I do not wish to repeat.

# On the Tübingen Writing.

### §. 29.

Regarding **Eusebius**; — page 229. <note>original: "quid ipse de apocalypsi senserit, haud facile quisquam certo definiverit; usque adeo in omnes partes se vertit."</note> *what he himself thought of the Apocalypse, no one could easily determine with certainty; so much does he turn in all directions.* How is it then simultaneously true that he **regarded the refutation** and the **contradiction** against the divinity of this book only as **empty dreams** and held them to be unfounded? <note>original: "nullo in loco habuit?"</note> *held them in no regard?* So the Mr. Chancellor spoke shortly before; and how does this also agree with page 231, <note>original: "fateor autem ego, probabilius fere esse... cum Dionysio suo... eum sensisse, et maluisse — Iohannem presbyterum pro auctore libri haberi etc."</note> *I confess, however, that it is almost more probable* (so one can indeed determine it,) *that he felt the same as his Dionysius* (why not also *with the 22 bishops of the Synod of Laodicea*) *and preferred — to have John the Presbyter <note>A figure distinct from John the Apostle in early church tradition.</note> regarded as the author of the book, etc.* Nevertheless, **Eusebius** is supposed to have held such <note>original: "somnia in nullo loco"</note> *dreams in no regard*, and not respected them at all. In Mr. **Stroth's** <note>Friedrich Andreas Stroth (1750–1785), a classical scholar and theologian.</note> treatise, this has been set forth much more clearly.

# Answer

The blame for the constant recommendations of incessant miracles, which were supposedly performed by the relics, really lies with these men; among whom Ambrose and Augustine are the least to be forgotten. <note>Ambrose (c. 340–397) and Augustine (354–430) were influential Latin Church Fathers who promoted the veneration of saints and relics.</note> However, it is quite certainly wrong that Gregory of Nazianzus and Gregory of Nyssa are also counted among these witnesses; especially since the former says quite clearly in his iambic verses that the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation</note> does not belong among the authentic books. I pass over the remaining names; I admit that *the number of those who counted the Apocalypse among the sacred and divine books is far greater than the number of those who referred it to the apocryphal works* <note>original: "longe maiorem eorum qui libris sacris et diuinis Apocalypsin annumerarunt, quam illorum, qui ad apocryphos eum retulerunt, numerum esse"</note>; but I cannot admit that there is also *greater authority* <note>original: "maiorem esse auctoritatem"</note>. From what, then, should the *greater authority* of the great multitude—whom the Greeks call <term>the many</term> <note>original: "τȣς πολλȣς" (hoi polloi); refers to the common masses or the majority without scholarly weight</note>—be understood here? 

Furthermore, Jerome undeniably spoke *from the stronger side* <note>original: "a potiori"; meaning he spoke based on the prevailing or more significant evidence</note>, and he certainly did not prefer the most insignificant or contemptible part, as he himself had been acquainted with scholarship, when he writes *to Dardanus*: *with the same liberty the churches of the Greeks do not receive the Apocalypse among the canonical scriptures.* <note>original: "eadem libertate Graecorum ecclesiae non suscipiunt apocalypsin inter canonicas scripturas." Jerome (c. 347–420) was the translator of the Vulgate Bible.</note> Therefore, there must have been neither a *greater number* nor *greater authority* among the Greek teachers *in favor of the Apocalypse*, if Jerome says the churches of the Greeks do not accept the Apocalypse among the canonical writings. Now, in the first sentence, was the word "Latins" an indication that only a few and quite insignificant Latin churches were to be understood? Do the "fewer and less noble" constitute what Jerome calls the "custom of the Latins"? On the contrary, he speaks of those who are greater in number and authority, and he leaves out the few and ignoble entirely and does not count them; he is therefore speaking of the majority of Latin churches and their Canon in his time. Consequently, the following clause also speaks of the majority and most prominent Greek churches; to them, he attributes this public custom at that time of leaving the Apocalypse out of the canon. Now I safely let all readers judge whether my interpretation—as previously with Gaius <note>referring to the early Christian writer Gaius of Rome</note>—is not once again the true and correct one. And if I am right here: then Jerome’s report is more valuable to me, stating that the Greek churches at that time did not [accept] the Apocalypse.

Regarding the <term>canonical reading<note>original: "lectionem canonicam"; refers to the official list of scripture passages authorized for public reading in church</note></term>, this rests upon the <term>Synods<note>Ecclesiastical councils or gatherings of church leaders to decide on doctrine or administration</note></term>; the custom of *Athanasius* <note>Athanasius of Alexandria (c. 296–373 AD), a prominent Church Father known for his list of New Testament books</note> and others is a different matter. The objections against the authority of **Papias, Justin,** and **Irenaeus** <note>Early Church Fathers from the 2nd century AD</note>. They held many **errors** <note>original: "errores"</note>. I believe it is easy to recognize that one does not mean **errors** in the sense that they are a constant attribute of all human beings; rather, one means those particular incorrect opinions or disorders in the previous exercise of the intellect and will, which render a **Subject** <note>The individual observer or thinker</note> incapable of undertaking the necessary, serious investigation of the matters which they wish to affirm and certify for others. For example: simple-minded people affirm, as they believe from certain experience, that they have seen ghosts here or there, or even the Devil himself. Here it is clear that their own errors have become the sources of their supposed experience. One is deceived here by the distinction between **dogmatic errors** <note>original: "erroribus dogmaticis"</note> and **historical matters** <note>original: "rebus historicis"</note>; it is argued that from the former one cannot infer **errors in historical matters**; but the question now concerns...

# Answer

Regarding the divinity of the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation</note> as a historical matter: I do not wish to be long-winded; but (a) it is certainly within the right of any reader of the writings of **Irenaeus** and **Justin Martyr** to judge the state of mind of these authors themselves. As soon as we find them to be partly ignorant and fanatical, and partly also quite eager to spread and recommend the contents of the Revelation, then they become witnesses in their own cause <note>original: "testes in propria caussa"; a legal principle meaning their testimony is biased because they have a personal stake in the outcome</note>. (b) I deny that the question concerning the inspiration of the Apocalypse concerns a **History**, in the usual sense of the word **History**. Even the most foolish people can hear and see what happens externally; but when a divine **Inspiration** is communicated to an author, no one can hear or see it, because it concerns an internal event and not an external one. Page 244: one might oppose this with the **silence** of other writers, such as **Ignatius, Polycarp, and Papias.** Answer: how many reasons could there have been why someone might not have made mention of the Apocalypse, even when a convenient opportunity presented itself? <note>original: "quam multae possunt fuisse rationes; ob quas apocalypseos, etiam oblata occasione commoda, mentionem aliquis non fecerit."</note> Certainly, there could have been many reasons why some writers do not cite this book, even when there was a very fitting opportunity to do so; there could indeed be many reasons for this. But now, the defenders of the Apocalypse ought to prove their nonetheless affirmed proposition: namely, that these writers who remain silent nevertheless knew the Apocalypse and themselves—
