# On the Tübingen Writing.

even if they had accepted it as divine, they simply chose—for all sorts of other reasons (and not at all out of rejection)—not to bring this book to the attention of their readers, wishing to prevent future doubts by providing a clear report of it. I only fear that among the many reasons, this one specifically remained: these authors did not accept this Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation</note> as a divine book; its history was suspicious or even intolerable to them; therefore, they did not want to help ensure it would be read further by their friends at that time, to whom they otherwise wrote quite extensively. And, if the opposition to this book was a sin, and a misfortune for others could have arisen back then: then surely no other reasons were strong enough to advise them to remain silent in the face of such misfortune and evil for the Christians, who stood in danger of losing a divine book. It is not necessary for me to write more about this.

# Answer

### §. 36.

The dogmatic reasons that have been used particularly in recent times against this book are small, indeed of no importance <note>original: "parui, imo nullius momenti"</note>; and those readers who investigate everything precisely and with God-fearing devotion (the word used was *religiose*; it does not mean "superstitiously"), would see it: most of these arguments rely on nothing but precarious hypotheses <note>original: "pleraque non nisi precariis hypothesibus niti"</note>; which shall be further demonstrated. If, however, both Oeder <note>Georg Ludwig Oeder (1694–1760), a critic of the authenticity of the Apocalypse</note> and I have brought forward nothing but things of no importance, then on one hand it was not necessary to complain about the "very dangerous enemies" of the Apocalypse, and on the other hand, such an extensive refutation was not necessary, and Mr. Professor Schmid had no reason at all to sound such a loud theological alarm.

I will point it out to the Mr. Chancellor. They were merely incapable, ignorant people; later, the literal, fanatical explanation was discarded, and a spiritual one was introduced. In that case, it is true, one can now recount spiritual truths; but the question remains: did the author think this way, and thus write in the spirit of Christ?

# Appendix.

I have undertaken this in a thousand (or rather, in all) passages of the **Church Fathers** <note>The influential early Christian theologians of the first few centuries</note>. This time, however, the attempt did not succeed, *to whitewash all the walls with one bucket of paint* <note>original: "vna fidelia omnes dealbare parietes"; a Latin proverb meaning to solve all problems with a single, perhaps superficial, stroke</note>. Meanwhile, some readers—if they continue to follow the Senior <note>A title for a senior Lutheran clergyman or superintendent</note> diligently—will see that I am a blasphemer because I have abolished **demonic possessions** from the whole of theology and now, as it appears, even in Lower Saxony. And whoever is such a blasphemer is also a falsifier of the **Fathers** <note>original: "patrum"</note>; he is—whatever the Senior himself wishes to make of him. 

Only the judgment of other Christians, who know their own freedom, remains unbound; and all honest teachers help to ensure that this freedom and skill of Christians grows from day to day. Therefore, when the Pope rushes in with his sword and says: "I want you to give me your faith; I and my brothers have instituted it thus; it was established by a forced council," and so on. But my faith rests only on Christ and his word (not on the speeches and opinions of other persons often recounted in the Gospel); not on the Pope, nor on councils. Therefore, I must insist on the Gospel with all my might <note>original: "omnibus, quod aiunt vngulis," literally "with all my claws/nails"</note>, disregarding the precepts of all men. For my faith is the judge here, so that I dare to pronounce: this doctrine is good and true; that one is harmful and false. To this judgment even the Pope is subject, along with his entire retinue; indeed, all...

# Appendix.

...men. Therefore, those who say that *the judgment of the scriptures rests solely with the holy fathers and the pope* lie most shamelessly. And if the servant girl of the lowliest miller, or a nine-year-old boy endowed with faith, should judge according to the Gospel <insert>namely, whether a certain doctrine be good and true, even if the Jews held it to be so, or if this or that theologian previously judged it in such a way</insert>: the pope ought to obey this person and prostrate himself at their feet, if only he wishes to be counted in the register of Christians, etc.
